The chapter argues that complex knowing requires practitioners to make theoretical distinctions explicit, examine their own assumptions, and accept that interventions may have unintended effects. Humility is not indecision or a rejection of evidence. It is disciplined awareness that conclusions are provisional, other perspectives may reveal omissions, and practice must be monitored and revised as circumstances develop.
Study instruction:
- "After designing an intervention, list the assumptions most likely to fail and specify what evidence would trigger a revision."
Key point:
- Complexity makes complete prediction and certainty difficult to justify.
- Self-critique and willingness to revise are practical requirements of responsible intervention.
12. Q: What is systemic pluralism, the second commitment of CST?
A: Show answer
Systemic pluralism holds that decision-making and intervention are improved by using multiple perspectives and a range of methodologies and methods. Different approaches illuminate different features because they rest on different assumptions and are suited to different kinds of difficulty. Pluralism does not mean combining methods indiscriminately or treating all interpretations as equally useful. It means making informed choices about which perspectives and tools can complement one another, while remaining aware of their limitations. The chapter describes an ideal type that is multiperspectival, multimethodological, multimethod, and sustained throughout an intervention.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how two methods with different assumptions could provide complementary insight into one intervention."
Key point:
- Pluralism uses difference as a resource for understanding and action.
- Method combinations should be justified by critique and the needs of the situation.
13. Q: What does a multiperspectival approach require in practice?
A: Show answer
A multiperspectival approach deliberately considers relevant worldviews and systemic perspectives before deciding how to intervene. It seeks a richer understanding than one viewpoint could provide. A service redesign, for example, may be understood differently by service users, frontline staff, managers, regulators, and affected communities. Each perspective can reveal distinct objectives, constraints, risks, or measures of success. The task is not simply to collect opinions; it is to understand how perspectives frame the system and what each makes visible. The learning must then inform action rather than remain an unconnected consultation exercise.
Study instruction:
- "Map the key stakeholder worldviews in a service redesign and record the distinct problem definitions and success criteria each contributes."
Key point:
- Multiperspectival practice broadens understanding through relevant worldviews.
- Perspective-taking should influence intervention design rather than become token consultation.
14. Q: What is multimethodological pluralism?
A: Show answer
Multimethodological pluralism means combining different systems methodologies because each is grounded in distinct assumptions and offers different ways of intervening. A technical modelling method may help examine resource flows, a soft systems approach may explore contested meanings, and a critical approach may expose unequal power. Systemic critique helps practitioners understand the contribution and limitations of each methodology. The combination should be justified by the situation and the role each method plays. It is not a requirement to use every methodology in every project.
Study instruction:
- "For a complex organizational change, explain the distinct contribution expected from each proposed systems methodology."
Key point:
- Different methodologies can complement one another when their assumptions and limits are understood.
- A combination should be selected for its contribution, not for variety alone.
15. Q: What does multimethod pluralism add to the use of systems methodologies?
A: Show answer
Multimethod pluralism is broader than combining named systems methodologies. It encourages flexible use of relevant methods, models, tools, and techniques within an intervention. These may include interviews, workshops, document analysis, quantitative analysis, simulation, observation, and scenario work. The chapter emphasizes this flexibility because practitioners must respond to the uniqueness of situations and to demands that emerge as an intervention progresses. A practitioner may therefore use several practical methods without adopting several complete methodologies. The choice should remain purposeful and context-sensitive.
Study instruction:
- "Classify the tools in a proposed intervention and explain how each contributes to inquiry or action."
Key point:
- Multimethodology combines systems methodologies; multimethod practice draws on a wider toolkit.
- Tool selection should respond to context and emerging needs.
16. Q: Why does CST favor parallel rather than purely serial use of different approaches?
A: Show answer
A serial approach assigns one methodology to each phase of an intervention, such as using a soft approach at the beginning and a structured design method later. CST warns that this can imply that perspectives, political relationships, and power issues have been settled permanently during the early phase. Parallel use keeps relevant approaches available throughout the intervention, so interpretive and critical concerns remain visible during design, implementation, and review. Parallel use does not mean every method must operate with equal intensity at every moment; it means that important perspectives are not treated as issues that can simply be completed and forgotten.
Study instruction:
- "Review a phased intervention plan and identify stakeholder or power issues that could re-emerge after the initial diagnosis."
Key point:
- Parallel pluralism maintains relevant perspectives throughout an intervention.
- Early agreement does not guarantee that political and interpretive issues have disappeared.
17. Q: What is systemic improvement, the third commitment of CST?
A: Show answer
Systemic improvement is a broad conception of making a situation better, not simply increasing efficiency or achieving a narrow target. The chapter draws on ideals associated with plenty, truth, moral good, and freedom, and includes effectiveness, mutual understanding, resilience, antifragility, fairness, and sustainability. The relevant criteria depend on the situation and the people affected. This broader view prevents an intervention from being judged successful merely because one performance measure improves while other important human or systemic consequences deteriorate.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed change using operational, ethical, social, resilience, and sustainability criteria rather than a single output measure."
Key point:
- Systemic improvement includes social and ethical dimensions as well as operational performance.
- Success criteria should reflect the situation and the people who benefit or bear costs.
18. Q: How does CST distinguish improving a situation from improving the means of achieving improvement?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies two related aims. The first is to improve the actual situation in which an intervention takes place, such as a service, organization, or societal problem. The second is to improve the means by which practitioners bring about improvement, including the knowledge, methods, collaboration, and learning processes used. An intervention may produce a local benefit yet teach little about future practice. Conversely, a methodological innovation may be intellectually interesting without materially helping people facing the problem. CST seeks both practical change and reflective development of the means used to create change.
Study instruction:
- "For a completed project, assess both the change in the target situation and what the team learned about its intervention methods."
Key point:
- CST evaluates practical outcomes and the learning that improves future interventions.
- Methods should contribute to better practice, not only to theoretical discussion.
19. Q: What is action research, and why is it central to systemic improvement?
A: Show answer
Action research seeks to contribute both to practical concerns in an immediate problematic situation and to the development of social-science knowledge through collaboration within an agreed ethical framework. It links inquiry with action rather than separating research from practice. The chapter traces the term to Kurt Lewin and emphasizes cooperation by people engaged in the situation, including their participation in monitoring and assessing results. For CST, action research offers a way to improve the actual situation while learning how interventions can be conducted more effectively. It also makes the ethical conditions of inquiry explicit.
Study instruction:
- "Design an action-research cycle linking a practical problem, collaborative inquiry, intervention, observation, reflection, and revised action."
Key point:
- Action research joins practical problem-solving with learning and knowledge development.
- Participation and an agreed ethical framework are central to the approach.
20. Q: How did Kurt Lewin shape the action-research tradition used in CST?
A: Show answer
Lewin argued that social research should serve practice and that research producing only books was insufficient. Research in an organization should be guided by its needs and help define those needs more precisely. People involved should cooperate and participate in monitoring and assessing outcomes. The chapter emphasizes that this participation need not undermine scientific rigor. Lewin's influence supports a view of research as a collaborative process in which knowledge develops through engagement with real situations. CST draws on this tradition to connect intervention outcomes with reflective learning about the methods used.
Study instruction:
- "Identify how participants can help define an organizational problem, take part in action, and assess the results."
Key point:
- Lewin linked social inquiry to organizational needs and practical action.
- Collaboration can support both practical relevance and disciplined learning.
21. Q: Why must action research include collaboration and an ethical framework?
A: Show answer
People within a situation possess knowledge about its history, relationships, constraints, and consequences that outside analysts may not have. Their participation helps define the problem and assess whether an intervention has genuinely improved it. An ethical framework is necessary because inquiry and intervention can affect people's opportunities, resources, dignity, and power. Collaboration should involve more than asking for comments after decisions have been made. Participants need an appropriate role in defining needs, interpreting evidence, and reviewing outcomes. The chapter treats collaboration and ethics as conditions for responsible inquiry, not optional additions.
Study instruction:
- "For a workplace intervention, identify participants, their influence on decisions, and safeguards for consent, fairness, and confidentiality."
Key point:
- Participation improves contextual understanding and assessment of consequences.
- Ethical arrangements must govern how people are involved and how intervention effects are handled.
22. Q: How can action research maintain rigor while remaining practical and participatory?
A: Show answer
Rigor comes from making the inquiry process explicit, gathering and examining evidence carefully, documenting decisions, and reflecting systematically on outcomes. Practical relevance comes from grounding the work in organizational needs and involving people who understand the situation. These aims need not conflict. Participants can help identify the problem and assess results while researchers maintain transparent methods, question assumptions, and distinguish evidence from interpretation. The chapter refers to Checkland and Holwell's framework for action research in systems thinking. The essential discipline is to learn from action without treating one local result as universal proof.
Study instruction:
- "Specify the evidence, decision records, participant roles, and reflection process needed to make an intervention auditable and learnable."
Key point:
- Practical involvement does not require abandoning methodological discipline.
- Documented inquiry and critical reflection connect local learning with wider knowledge.
23. Q: Why does the chapter criticize the systems movement's uneven record in developing theory and methodology?
A: Show answer
The chapter acknowledges that systems thinking has often improved the situations in which it has been used. It is less satisfied with the record of systems communities in developing their own theories and methodologies. Some communities have remained attached to favored worldviews and methods despite evidence from practice that other approaches may be more useful. This tendency contributed to divisions and disputes. The criticism is that methodological loyalty can become self-protective: practitioners defend a school rather than learn from the situation. CST's commitments are intended to keep theory and practice open to critique, plural learning, and improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Find an example where a preferred method was retained despite weak results and identify what evidence should prompt a change."
Key point:
- Methodological loyalty can prevent learning from practical evidence.
- A systems community should revise its theories and methods when experience warrants it.
24. Q: Why does the chapter argue that systemic pragmatism is needed in a volatile, uncertain, complex, and ambiguous world?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes contemporary conditions as volatile, uncertain, complex, and ambiguous, with interacting dimensions of ontological and cognitive complexity. Situations change, elements are heterogeneous, and outcomes may be difficult to predict. A philosophy that demands one secure theoretical foundation before action can delay learning or create unjustified confidence. Systemic pragmatism offers a way to justify action through informed judgment about what is likely to work in context, while retaining critique, pluralism, and reflection. It does not promise certainty; it supports responsible action when knowledge is incomplete and revision is necessary.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a decision where evidence is incomplete and explain how provisional action, monitoring, and revision could be more responsible than waiting for certainty."
Key point:
- Systemic pragmatism responds to uncertainty through practical judgment and learning.
- Action should remain open to revision as conditions and evidence change.
25. Q: Why did the early metaparadigm justification for CST become problematic?
A: Show answer
Early CST presented itself as a metaparadigm grounded in Habermas's theory of human interests, allowing it to stand above different paradigms and select methods according to technical, practical, or emancipatory interests. The chapter later questions this position because observers are not outside the reality they examine. A supposedly superior framework cannot simply hover above competing perspectives and judge them from a neutral position. CST therefore considered alternatives such as critique between paradigms and reflective conversation. The challenge was to retain pluralism without relying on an unquestionable theoretical position above all others.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a framework claiming to stand above all paradigms may reproduce the privileged-observer problem that CST seeks to critique."
Key point:
- CST cannot assume that its own framework occupies a neutral position outside the world.
- Pluralism needs justification that does not depend on an unquestioned metaparadigm.
26. Q: What are discordant pluralism and reflective conversation between paradigms?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses Gregory's discordant pluralism and Morgan's reflective conversation as alternatives to a single metaparadigm. Instead of placing one framework above all others, paradigms can critique one another using their different assumptions and insights. No paradigm is allowed to escape examination simply because it claims authority. The approach accepts that disagreement may persist and that perspectives may remain incompatible in some respects. Its value lies in keeping inquiry open to challenge. However, CST must still explain how it can make judgments without collapsing into the view that every position is equally acceptable.
Study instruction:
- "Use two contrasting paradigms to critique each other's assumptions, omissions, strengths, and implications for action."
Key point:
- Reflective conversation uses differences among paradigms as a continuing source of critique.
- Pluralism still requires reasoned judgment rather than automatic acceptance of every position.
27. Q: What is pragmatism in the philosophical tradition described by the chapter?
A: Show answer
Pragmatism connects ideas, knowledge, and belief with practical activity and the consequences of acting. The chapter traces antecedents to practical wisdom and later philosophical debates, then concentrates on Peirce, James, and Dewey. Pragmatists reject the idea that knowledge is simply a spectator's passive representation of a finished world. Ideas are tools people develop to cope with situations, and their significance becomes clearer through use and inquiry. Pragmatism does not claim that every useful belief is permanently true. It emphasizes fallibility, experience, action, and the need to revise beliefs as the world and our understanding change.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize pragmatism using the connected ideas of experience, practical consequences, fallibility, and revision."
Key point:
- Pragmatism connects ideas with action and the consequences of using them.
- Knowledge develops through engagement with changing situations rather than detached certainty.
28. Q: What is the spectator theory of knowledge, and why do pragmatists reject it?
A: Show answer
The spectator theory imagines the knower as standing apart from the world and seeking an accurate representation of an external reality. The chapter contrasts this with pragmatism, which treats people as participants in the world they seek to understand. Ideas are not merely pictures waiting to be verified from outside; they are instruments developed to guide action and cope with problematic situations. Dewey's account makes knowledge a mode of participation whose value depends partly on how effectively it helps people interact with their environment. Inquiry is therefore situated, and the observer's activity influences what is known.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how a manager's participation in a change process may shape both the evidence gathered and the understanding of the problem."
Key point:
- Pragmatists see knowers as participants rather than detached spectators.
- Knowledge develops through interaction between people, ideas, and their environments.
29. Q: What is fallibilism, and how does it affect systems practice?
A: Show answer
Fallibilism is the view, associated in the chapter with Peirce, that beliefs and conclusions remain open to correction. A conclusion may be sufficiently supported to guide action while still being revisable if further inquiry reveals weaknesses. This position avoids both dogmatism and paralysis: practitioners need not wait for certainty before acting, but they should not treat a current conclusion as immune to challenge. In systems practice, fallibilism encourages explicit assumptions, monitoring, feedback, and willingness to change an intervention when evidence or circumstances change. It supports learning without implying that all claims are equally credible.
Study instruction:
- "Take a decision based on incomplete evidence and record what new evidence would cause you to revise it."
Key point:
- Fallible conclusions can guide action without being treated as final truths.
- Monitoring and revision are necessary when acting on provisional knowledge.
30. Q: What is Peirce's pragmatic maxim, and how can it guide systems practice?
A: Show answer
Peirce's pragmatic maxim asks practitioners to consider the conceivable practical effects of an idea and treat their understanding of those effects as central to understanding the idea itself. Applied to systems practice, this means clarifying what difference a concept or model would make in actual decisions and interventions. A proposed framework should be examined not only for abstract elegance but also for the actions, expectations, and consequences it implies. The maxim encourages explicit reasoning about practical implications so that vague concepts can be tested against what they lead people to do.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a systems concept and state the practical consequences that would follow if a decision-maker accepted it."
Key point:
- The pragmatic maxim connects the meaning of an idea to its conceivable practical effects.
- Concepts should be examined through the actions and consequences they imply.
31. Q: How does William James extend pragmatism toward the consequences of beliefs in people's lives?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that James broadened the pragmatic maxim by asking what definite difference a particular view of the world makes in people's lives at particular moments. This moves evaluation beyond abstract definitions toward lived consequences. A belief may shape priorities, relationships, decisions, and willingness to act. James also treats reality as changing and plural rather than complete and fixed, so the usefulness and truth of an idea must be understood through ongoing experience. For CST, this supports attention to how systems theories affect the people involved and whether they help address the concrete concerns that prompted an intervention.
Study instruction:
- "For a policy assumption, identify how accepting it would change decisions and experiences for different affected groups."
Key point:
- James emphasizes the consequences of beliefs for actual human lives.
- The practical meaning of an idea is revealed through its effects in particular circumstances.
32. Q: What are the five themes of pragmatism identified in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies five connected themes: rejection of the spectator theory of knowledge; fallibilism and the absence of final certainty; evaluation of beliefs through practical consequences; acceptance of pluralism and multiple ways of describing reality; and understanding theories as instruments for action and change. Together they form a philosophy of engaged inquiry. People participate in the situations they study, use ideas to guide action, test those ideas through experience, and remain ready to revise them. These themes support CST's interest in critique, pluralism, improvement, and a practical philosophical foundation.
Study instruction:
- "List the five themes and connect each one to a concrete decision or intervention practice."
Key point:
- Pragmatism combines participation, fallibility, consequences, pluralism, and instrumental theories.
- These themes provide a philosophical basis for practical and revisable systems inquiry.
33. Q: How does the chapter connect pragmatism with Bogdanov, von Bertalanffy, and Norbert Wiener?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that pragmatist ideas were already present in important strands of systems thinking, even when some thinkers also pursued ambitions for a universal science. Bogdanov treated philosophical truth as a tool for guiding human practice. Von Bertalanffy's perspective philosophy held that forms of knowledge capture only certain aspects of truth and emphasized the creative role of cognition. Wiener was influenced by Dewey and James, and his work can be read as part of a pragmatist tradition in which knowledge emerges through interaction and action. These connections present systemic pragmatism as a development of systems thinking's practical roots.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the contributions of Bogdanov, von Bertalanffy, and Wiener in relation to practical knowledge and the limits of representation."
Key point:
- Pragmatist themes have antecedents within the systems tradition itself.
- Knowledge is situated, active, and connected to human practice.
34. Q: What role did Churchman play in connecting pragmatism, ethics, and systems thinking?
A: Show answer
Churchman applied Singer's pragmatist philosophy to practical problems and emphasized the responsibility of inquiry to improve the human condition. He argued that systems are products of observers and that boundary choices have ethical consequences because they influence whose interests a design serves. He urged practitioners to include viewpoints often treated as outside the systems approach, such as politics, morality, religion, and aesthetics. His idea of unfolding the implications of partial perspectives and sweeping in more viewpoints supports continuing examination of system boundaries and purposes.
Study instruction:
- "For a system design, identify who benefits from the current boundary and which excluded viewpoints should be brought into inquiry."
Key point:
- Churchman linked systems inquiry to ethical responsibility and the human condition.
- Boundary judgments shape whose interests are recognized and whose may be neglected.
35. Q: What are boundary judgments, and why are they ethically significant?
A: Show answer
Boundary judgments are decisions about what is included within a system of interest and what is treated as outside it. These choices shape which people, effects, resources, and consequences count in an analysis. They are ethically significant because a design may appear successful for the selected system while transferring costs to people or environments excluded from the boundary. Churchman's approach asks practitioners to unfold the implications of partial judgments and sweep in as many relevant viewpoints as possible. Boundaries are necessary for analysis, but they should remain visible, discussable, and open to challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the boundary of a proposed service system and list affected people, externalities, and long-term consequences that may have been excluded."
Key point:
- System boundaries are choices with practical and ethical consequences.
- Critical inquiry tests who and what has been included or excluded from analysis.
36. Q: How did Ackoff's interactive planning differ from a conventional predict-and-prepare approach?
A: Show answer
Ackoff rejected the idea that social systems science achieves objectivity simply by constructing value-free models and testing them against an external reality. He argued that objectivity can be approached through interaction among groups with diverse values, making inquiry value-full rather than value-free. His interactive planning approach emphasized designing a desirable future and inventing ways to bring it about, instead of only predicting what may happen and preparing to respond. This places human agency, participation, and shared purpose at the centre of planning and aligns with systemic improvement.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a forecast-led plan with an interactive planning exercise that begins by describing a desirable future and working toward action."
Key point:
- Interactive planning emphasizes co-creating a desirable future.
- Diverse values and human agency are central to planning social systems.
37. Q: How does pragmatism influence the chapter's view of systems design?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents design as richer than solving a narrowly defined problem. Design involves imagining a state of affairs that does not yet exist and bringing it into concrete form as a purposeful addition to the world. This requires systems thinkers to combine perspectives, consider human values, and move between different images of the system. A design may be technically feasible yet still fail to be desirable, fair, or sustainable. Pragmatism treats theories and models as tools for shaping action, while critique and pluralism help designers examine assumptions and alternatives. Design is therefore practical, ethical, and imaginative.
Study instruction:
- "For a service redesign, define a desirable future state, the values it should embody, and the perspectives needed to develop it."
Key point:
- Systems design creates desirable possibilities, not only remedies for defects.
- Good design integrates feasibility with values, perspectives, and consequences.
38. Q: How can practitioners integrate systemic critique, pluralism, improvement, and pragmatism in one intervention?
A: Show answer
A practitioner begins by examining how the problem has been framed and whose experiences or interests may be missing. Relevant perspectives are then gathered, and complementary methodologies and methods are selected through critique. These approaches remain available throughout the intervention rather than being confined to separate phases. Improvement criteria are defined broadly with affected participants, including operational and ethical dimensions. Action proceeds through collaborative inquiry, while evidence and consequences are monitored. Pragmatism guides judgment when knowledge is incomplete: decisions are justified by their contextual purposes and expected effects, then revised as learning develops. The process is iterative because new information can change both the problem definition and the appropriate methods.
Study instruction:
- "Apply all four commitments to a live organizational issue and document how each changes diagnosis, method choice, success criteria, and review."
Key point:
- The four commitments reinforce one another rather than forming a one-time linear sequence.
- Critique, pluralism, improvement, and pragmatic learning should recur throughout an intervention.
39. Q: What are the principal achievements of CST identified in the chapter, and what challenge remains?
A: Show answer
The conclusion identifies three established achievements. CST has provided a thorough critique of systems approaches and methodologies, showing their theoretical and practical strengths and weaknesses. It has made a strong case for pluralism, helping move the systems movement beyond destructive paradigm wars. It has also clarified systemic improvement and brought disadvantage, marginalization, and empowerment into the discussion of better systems. A further achievement is proposed in the establishment of pragmatism as a philosophical foundation for systems thinking and practice. The chapter also notes that CST needs to become more accessible to decision-makers without distorting its central premises; the following part of the book develops Critical Systems Practice.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize CST's achievements and explain why practical accessibility must not remove its critical and pluralist foundations."
Key point:
- CST's achievements concern critique, pluralism, and a broader conception of improvement.
- Its future development requires a practical form that preserves the depth of its underlying commitments.
Rows: 39
Table 4
Critical Systems Practice: EPIC, Multiperspectival Inquiry, and Adaptive Intervention
1. Q: What is Critical Systems Practice (CSP), and what problem is it designed to address?
A: Show answer
Critical Systems Practice is a multiperspectival and multimethodological approach for improving complex situations in which technical, economic, organisational, human, cultural, political and environmental factors interact. It is designed for “messes” and wicked problems where boundaries are contested, stakeholders disagree, uncertainty is high and no single model can represent the whole situation. CSP does not promise a final, universal solution. Instead, it helps participants explore a situation through different systemic lenses, select methodologies and methods that fit the issues, intervene flexibly, evaluate consequences from several perspectives and agree what to do next. Its purpose is both practical improvement and development of the systems-thinking capabilities of participants.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a routine best-practice problem and a complex policy mess require different approaches."
Key point:
- CSP is for complex, contested situations, not for every routine problem.
- The method combines multiple perspectives, methods, action and reflection.
Source links:
-
- Boulding: General Systems Theory—The Skeleton of Science
2. Q: How did CSP develop from Total Systems Intervention (TSI)?
A: Show answer
TSI was the first critical-systems multimethodology, published by Flood and Jackson in 1991. It treated systems methodologies as resources that could be organised and employed according to their strengths and weaknesses in planning, design, problem-solving and evaluation. It aimed at efficiency, effectiveness, viability, mutual understanding, empowerment and emancipation. Later criticism of totalising discourse, the loss of confidence in a single guiding metaparadigm, and lessons from chaos and complexity theory made a rethink necessary. CSP emerged as a more modest and adaptive approach to learning within the unknowable. The chapter notes that the original TSI name was ultimately judged misleading because the intended process was critical learning rather than a totalising system of control.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the original ambition of TSI with the more adaptive orientation of CSP."
Key point:
- CSP preserves multimethodological learning while rejecting totalising certainty.
- The historical shift reflects postmodern, complexity and practical lessons.
3. Q: What does multiperspectival mean in CSP?
A: Show answer
Multiperspectival means that a complex situation is examined through several systemic perspectives rather than through one privileged way of seeing. CSP identifies five perspectives—mechanical, interrelationships, organismic, purposeful and societal/environmental. Each highlights different concerns and makes different questions visible. A major project, for example, can be viewed as a machine for delivering a purpose, as a network of interacting parts, as an evolving organism, as a system of divergent purposes requiring accommodation, and as a system with distributional and environmental consequences. Multiperspectival analysis does not mean treating every view as equally useful in every circumstance; it means resisting premature closure and using perspectives deliberately.
Study instruction:
- "Take one organisational problem and write one diagnostic question from each of the five perspectives."
Key point:
- Different lenses reveal different failure modes and improvement opportunities.
- Breadth of perspective is a protection against narrow diagnosis.
4. Q: What does multimethodological mean in CSP?
A: Show answer
Multimethodological means that more than one systems methodology, model or method may be used in a coherent intervention, with each selected for what it can contribute to the issues identified. Engineering systems approaches may support mechanical concerns; system dynamics may help with interrelationships; living-systems approaches may illuminate adaptation and viability; soft systems approaches may address divergent purposes; and emancipatory approaches may expose marginalisation, power and environmental harm. CSP does not advocate indiscriminate method mixing. The sequence is to understand the issues, appreciate the strengths and weaknesses of available approaches, choose an appropriate starting combination and remain willing to revise it as the situation changes.
Study instruction:
- "List two methods that could complement each other and state the distinct contribution of each."
Key point:
- Multimethodology is purposeful combination, not methodological accumulation.
- Methods should be chosen for fit with the issues and their systemic assumptions.
5. Q: What is the EPIC framework?
A: Show answer
EPIC is the mnemonic for the four main stages of CSP: Explore, Produce, Intervene and Check. Explore develops a rich appreciation of the situation through five systemic perspectives and identifies primary and secondary issues. Produce creates an intervention strategy by selecting appropriate methodologies, models and methods, then structuring objectives and schedules. Intervene applies the chosen approaches flexibly while staying alert to change and revisiting earlier stages. Check evaluates improvements, reflects on learning and agrees next steps. EPIC is a guiding framework rather than a rigid linear algorithm; the stages are interdependent and are revisited in practice.
Study instruction:
- "Memorise the four stages and explain why the framework is iterative rather than a one-way sequence."
Key point:
- EPIC links diagnosis, design, action and reflection.
- The stages guide practice but do not eliminate uncertainty or iteration.
6. Q: Why is Explore the first stage of CSP?
A: Show answer
Explore is necessary because complex situations are often misclassified when decision-makers jump directly to familiar solutions. VUCA conditions—volatility, uncertainty, complexity and ambiguity—combine with stakeholder conflict to create messes whose boundaries and causal structure are unclear. By using five systemic perspectives before choosing an intervention, participants can identify issues that a narrow framing would miss and avoid proscribing methodologies that might later be needed. Exploration also distinguishes primary issues that demand immediate attention from secondary issues that should remain visible. The chapter argues that even apparently simple or complicated situations may reveal deeper complexity once examined.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a recent problem that seemed simple at first and identify what an Explore stage might have uncovered."
Key point:
- Early exploration reduces the risk of solving the wrong problem.
- The initial framing determines which methods and stakeholders become visible.
Source links:
-
- Boulding: General Systems Theory—The Skeleton of Science
7. Q: What are primary and secondary issues in CSP?
A: Show answer
Primary issues are the most important concerns emerging from the exploration of the situation of interest; they guide the initial intervention strategy. Secondary issues are relevant concerns that may not require immediate action but could become more important as the intervention unfolds. The distinction is not permanent. New evidence, changing conditions or unintended consequences can move a secondary issue into the primary set, or make an initial priority less urgent. Keeping secondary issues visible prevents the intervention from becoming locked into its first agenda and supports the revisiting of Stage 1 during Intervene.
Study instruction:
- "Create a two-level issue list for a complex project and state what evidence would cause a secondary issue to become primary."
Key point:
- Priorities are provisional in a changing system.
- Secondary issues are monitored, not discarded.
8. Q: What are the five systemic perspectives used in the Explore stage?
A: Show answer
The five perspectives are mechanical, interrelationships, organismic, purposeful and societal/environmental. The mechanical perspective asks about efficiency, efficacy, structure and reliable performance. The interrelationships perspective examines interactions, feedback, dependencies and unintended consequences. The organismic perspective considers adaptation, viability, resilience and development over time. The purposeful perspective focuses on different goals, values, stakeholder interpretations, accommodation and conflict resolution. The societal/environmental perspective asks who benefits or loses, which groups are marginalised, and what consequences arise for sustainability and the environment. Together they provide breadth without assuming that any one perspective captures the whole.
Study instruction:
- "Build a five-column diagnostic worksheet and place at least one issue under each perspective."
Key point:
- Each perspective foregrounds a different kind of concern.
- A balanced diagnosis requires more than technical performance.
9. Q: How does the mechanical perspective contribute to CSP?
A: Show answer
The mechanical perspective treats the system as something that can be designed or organised to achieve a purpose. It is useful for examining structure, process, efficiency, reliability, capacity, resource use and immediate efficacy. It can reveal bottlenecks, redundant steps, weak interfaces and performance failures. Its limitation is that it may understate adaptation, human meaning, power, conflict and environmental effects. CSP therefore uses it as one lens rather than as the complete explanation. In evaluation, questions include whether the intervention works as intended, uses resources proportionately and produces the required outputs.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a service process mechanically by identifying inputs, activities, outputs, bottlenecks and performance measures."
Key point:
- Mechanical analysis supports efficiency but cannot define the whole problem.
- A system may be efficient and still ineffective, unjust or unsustainable.
10. Q: How does the interrelationships perspective contribute to CSP?
A: Show answer
The interrelationships perspective examines how parts influence one another through networks, feedback, dependencies and interactions. It helps identify delays, reinforcing or balancing loops, unintended consequences, cross-boundary effects and risks created by local optimisation. A policy may improve one indicator while worsening another because the parts are coupled. This perspective is especially useful when outcomes emerge from interactions rather than from one isolated cause. Its limitation is that a model of relationships remains selective and may omit questions of purpose, power or values. CSP combines it with the other perspectives rather than treating a network representation as a complete system image.
Study instruction:
- "Map a problem as a causal loop or dependency network and identify one reinforcing and one balancing effect."
Key point:
- Outcomes often emerge from interactions, not isolated components.
- Relationship mapping should be tested against stakeholder and environmental evidence.
Source links:
-
- Boulding: General Systems Theory—The Skeleton of Science
11. Q: How does the organismic perspective contribute to CSP?
A: Show answer
The organismic perspective views systems as living, evolving or adaptive entities. It highlights viability, resilience, development, learning, identity and the capacity to respond to changing environments. This is important for organisations, communities and ecosystems where stability cannot be achieved by fixed design alone. A solution that performs well under current conditions may reduce the system's ability to adapt later. The perspective also encourages attention to life-cycle dynamics and the relation between the system and its environment. Its limitation is that organic metaphors can obscure power, structure or deliberate design if used carelessly.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate an organisation's resilience by identifying how it senses change, learns, adapts and recovers from shocks."
Key point:
- Viability concerns continued ability to exist and adapt.
- Short-term performance can conceal long-term fragility.
12. Q: How does the purposeful perspective contribute to CSP?
A: Show answer
The purposeful perspective recognises that stakeholders may hold different purposes, values and interpretations of what counts as improvement. It focuses on dialogue, accommodation, mutual understanding, conflict resolution and the legitimacy of decisions. A technically sound proposal can fail if affected groups do not recognise its purpose or consider the process unfair. The perspective does not mean that all preferences must be satisfied; it means that purposes should be surfaced, compared and negotiated rather than hidden behind apparently neutral technical models. Its limitation is that dialogue alone cannot remove material constraints or structural inequalities.
Study instruction:
- "Interview stakeholders to identify their different definitions of success and where accommodation may be possible."
Key point:
- A system can fail because purposes diverge, even when processes work.
- Make assumptions about goals explicit and contestable.
13. Q: How does the societal/environmental perspective contribute to CSP?
A: Show answer
This perspective examines distribution, marginalisation, power, sustainability and environmental effects. It asks who is absent from the discussion, who bears risks, who benefits from the intervention and what consequences are imposed on future generations or the natural environment. It prevents a narrow focus on system owners or dominant stakeholders from being mistaken for overall improvement. An intervention may be efficient and accepted by powerful actors while worsening inequality or ecological harm. The perspective's limitation is not that it is too broad, but that its concerns can be ignored when decision-makers define success only in financial or operational terms.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct an impact review that identifies winners, losers, excluded voices and environmental consequences."
Key point:
- Improvement must be judged by its distribution and wider effects.
- Marginalised stakeholders and the environment must be visible in evaluation.
14. Q: What is the difference between a situation of interest, a problem and a wicked problem in CSP?
A: Show answer
A situation of interest is the broad area in which participants perceive a need for improvement. A problem is a more defined difficulty within that situation. A wicked problem is a problem whose boundaries, causes, values, stakeholders and possible solutions are contested and evolving. CSP begins with the situation of interest because premature problem definition can exclude relevant issues and perspectives. Wicked problems are not merely difficult technical puzzles; they involve disagreement about what the problem is and what improvement would mean. Their formulation changes as learning occurs, which is why CSP treats diagnosis and intervention as interdependent.
Study instruction:
- "Take a policy issue and show how its definition changes when different stakeholders describe it."
Key point:
- Wickedness concerns contested framing as well as complexity.
- Do not assume that the first problem statement is neutral or complete.
15. Q: Why does CSP use the chemical metaphor for problem-solving?
A: Show answer
The chemical metaphor treats problems as recurring conditions that may be dissolved for a period without creating worse problems elsewhere. Unlike the puzzle metaphor, it does not assume a single correct solution that permanently eliminates the problem. This is more appropriate to VUCA situations and wicked problems where conditions shift, interventions interact and new issues emerge. The goal becomes finding catalysts that improve the situation for as long as possible while monitoring side effects and being prepared to intervene again. Reappearance is not automatically evidence of failure; it may be a natural feature of the system.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a one-time “solution” plan as a chemical-metaphor plan with monitoring, side effects and re-entry points."
Key point:
- Complex problems require ongoing stewardship, not a final answer.
- A recurring problem may indicate changing conditions rather than defective effort.
16. Q: When is CSP appropriate, and when is it unnecessary?
A: Show answer
CSP is appropriate when a situation is complex, cross-boundary, contested, uncertain or likely to produce wide ramifications. Examples include public-service reform, healthcare crises, inequality, environmental change, organisational crises, strategic rethinking and partnership redesign. It is unnecessary when there is agreement on what needs doing and a proven practice or simple technique can reliably achieve the result. However, the chapter warns that apparently simple or complicated situations can reveal hidden complexity once explored. A brief Explore stage is therefore often justified before deciding that a full CSP intervention is excessive.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five work problems as routine, complicated, complex or chaotic and justify whether CSP is proportionate."
Key point:
- Use the lightest method that fits the system's complexity.
- A short exploration can prevent costly misclassification.
17. Q: How can CSP be used for diagnosis?
A: Show answer
For diagnosis, CSP investigates why an existing system is producing unsatisfactory outcomes. It explores the situation through the five perspectives, identifies primary and secondary issues, chooses methodologies that can illuminate those issues and gathers evidence through intervention. Diagnosis is not a detached prelude that ends before action begins. Because understanding changes as participants intervene and the situation evolves, diagnostic learning continues during Intervene and Check. A diagnostic use of CSP should therefore preserve uncertainty, make assumptions explicit and avoid treating the first model as definitive.
Study instruction:
- "Apply CSP to a service failure and identify what evidence would be gathered before and during intervention."
Key point:
- Diagnosis in CSP is active and iterative.
- The purpose is to learn enough to improve the situation, not to produce a perfect static model.
18. Q: How can CSP be used for design?
A: Show answer
For design, CSP helps bring a new system, organisation or arrangement into being. The Explore stage considers the context, stakeholders, purposes, dependencies and risks before the design is fixed. Produce selects methodologies and methods appropriate to the design challenge. Intervene pilots, develops or implements the design flexibly, while Check evaluates its performance, viability, stakeholder effects and environmental consequences. This prevents design from being reduced to a technical specification that ignores adaptation, conflict, power or future conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a design brief for a new organisation and include all five systemic perspectives."
Key point:
- Good design includes purposes, relationships, viability and consequences.
- A designed system must be able to adapt after launch.
19. Q: What is the purpose of the Produce stage?
A: Show answer
Produce translates the findings of Explore into an intervention strategy. Participants appreciate the variety of systems approaches, choose methodologies that fit the primary issues, select supporting models and methods, and structure, schedule and set objectives for the intervention. The strategy should be specific enough to coordinate action but flexible enough to accommodate uncertainty. It should identify measures of success, responsibilities, resources and review points. Produce is therefore more than choosing a technique; it is designing a coherent path from diagnosis to action.
Study instruction:
- "Create a Produce-stage plan for a complex project with objectives, methods, sequencing, resources and review points."
Key point:
- Produce turns plural diagnosis into a feasible intervention strategy.
- Method choice must be linked to issues and success measures.
20. Q: How should systems methodologies be chosen in CSP?
A: Show answer
Methodologies should be chosen by considering what they are good at, what systemic perspective they privilege and which primary issues they can address. Engineering methodologies tend to privilege mechanical concerns; system dynamics focuses on interrelationships; living-systems approaches on organismic viability; soft systems on purposeful differences; and emancipatory approaches on societal and environmental issues. The choice should also consider the context, participants, available resources, timing and the possibility that more than one methodology may be needed. No methodology should be selected solely because it is familiar or fashionable.
Study instruction:
- "Map each primary issue to the methodology that can best illuminate it and explain possible gaps."
Key point:
- Choose methods for problem fit, not personal preference.
- A method's assumptions shape which issues it can see and address.
21. Q: What is the difference between a systems methodology, model and method?
A: Show answer
A systems methodology is an overall approach that guides how an intervention is framed and conducted. A systems model is a representation that helps participants understand structure, relationships, purposes or dynamics. A method is a more specific procedure or technique used to gather information, analyse an issue, communicate, decide or act. In CSP, methodologies provide direction, while models and methods support the work within that direction. Models and methods may come from outside the systems tradition if they are useful and compatible with the intervention's principles. Confusing the levels can lead to choosing a tool before clarifying the kind of inquiry required.
Study instruction:
- "Take one intervention and label its guiding methodology, models and methods."
Key point:
- Methodology sets the orientation; models represent; methods operationalise.
- A useful tool cannot compensate for an unsuitable overall approach.
22. Q: Why does CSP recommend starting with one or two methodologies in normal circumstances?
A: Show answer
Exploration may reveal many issues requiring different approaches, but running many methodologies simultaneously can cause confusion, upheaval, excessive demands on participants and resource problems. CSP therefore recommends beginning with one methodology, or possibly two, that address the largest number of primary issues. The intervention can then generate learning about what additional approaches are needed. This is a sequencing principle, not an absolute prohibition. Large crises may require parallel workstreams, but even then roles, objectives and coordination must be explicit.
Study instruction:
- "Design a staged method plan and explain why the initial combination is manageable."
Key point:
- Methodological ambition must be balanced with absorptive capacity.
- Start with a coherent core and expand when learning justifies it.
23. Q: What does flexible intervention mean in CSP?
A: Show answer
Flexible intervention means applying chosen methodologies, models and methods without treating their initial specification as permanent. Participants remain alert to changes in the situation, revisit the five perspectives and reconsider whether the selected approaches still fit. New priorities may surface, the context may shift or an intervention may create side effects. Flexibility is not improvisation without discipline; it is structured willingness to revise the intervention in light of evidence. It requires review points, clear decision rights, transparent documentation and a tolerance for changing course.
Study instruction:
- "Define three triggers that would cause a project team to revisit its intervention strategy."
Key point:
- Flexibility is disciplined adaptation to new information.
- Re-entry into earlier stages is a normal feature of CSP.
24. Q: Why are Stages 1 and 2 revisited during Intervene?
A: Show answer
Intervening changes the situation and produces new information. A method that seemed appropriate at the start may no longer fit, a secondary issue may become primary or an unintended consequence may appear. Re-exploring the situation prevents the team from acting on obsolete assumptions; re-producing the strategy ensures that methods, objectives and schedules remain relevant. The chapter describes this as a continuous multimethodological cycle, particularly during Intervene. Revisiting is therefore a mechanism for learning and control, not evidence that planning failed.
Study instruction:
- "Write a review agenda for a mid-intervention re-entry into Explore and Produce."
Key point:
- The intervention is part of the inquiry.
- Plans must evolve when the system and evidence evolve.
25. Q: What does the Check stage evaluate?
A: Show answer
Check evaluates improvement achieved, learning gained about the systems approaches used and the next steps agreed by decision-makers. Evaluation must invoke concerns from all five perspectives. Mechanical questions address efficiency and efficacy; interrelationships questions examine interactions and unintended consequences; organismic questions consider viability and resilience; purposeful questions address effectiveness, mutual understanding and conflict resolution; societal/environmental questions examine marginalised stakeholders, sustainability and ecological effects. A positive result under one lens is not sufficient if the intervention worsens outcomes elsewhere.
Study instruction:
- "Build a five-perspective evaluation scorecard for an intervention."
Key point:
- Check is multidimensional evaluation plus learning and next-step agreement.
- An intervention is not successful merely because one metric improves.
26. Q: Why must evaluation in CSP be multiperspectival?
A: Show answer
Complex interventions can create trade-offs. A project may improve efficiency but reduce resilience, meet formal objectives but deepen conflict, or deliver outputs while shifting costs to marginalised people or the environment. Multiperspectival evaluation makes these effects visible and prevents a single performance measure from defining success. It also allows participants to discuss differences in value and interpretation rather than hiding them in a composite score. The goal is balanced evaluation that supports continuous improvement, not a claim that all consequences can be reduced to one neutral number.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a real intervention and identify one benefit and one harm under each perspective."
Key point:
- Success is multidimensional and can contain contradictions.
- Evaluation should expose trade-offs instead of concealing them.
27. Q: What is meant by learning about the systems approaches used?
A: Show answer
Participants should reflect not only on whether the intervention improved the situation, but also on what they learned about the strengths, weaknesses, assumptions and practical use of the methodologies, models and methods employed. Exposure to several systemic perspectives can improve cognitive flexibility and help people choose better approaches in future work. Academic action research may extend the reflection to learning about the situation itself and the contribution of CSP. The learning should be carried forward through changed practice, not left as an abstract retrospective.
Study instruction:
- "Write a post-intervention reflection that separates learning about the situation from learning about the methods."
Key point:
- Methodological learning is an outcome in its own right.
- Reflection increases future capacity for complex intervention.
28. Q: What are Mode 1 and Mode 2 uses of CSP?
A: Show answer
In Mode 1, CSP governs the intervention and leads it along an orderly path through its stages. In Mode 2, the intervention is situation-driven and CSP appears intermittently to help participants reflect on what is happening or move the work forward. Mode 1 is useful when deliberate structure and explicit coordination are required. Mode 2 is useful when events are fluid and a formal framework would obstruct responsiveness. The distinction concerns how visibly and continuously CSP structures the work, not whether learning and critical reflection are abandoned.
Study instruction:
- "Give one example where Mode 1 is appropriate and one where Mode 2 is more realistic."
Key point:
- CSP can be a visible process or an intermittent reflective resource.
- The mode should fit the rhythm and volatility of the situation.
29. Q: Why is CSP described as an ideal type?
A: Show answer
An ideal type is a model of what good practice would require, even though real interventions may never reproduce it perfectly. EPIC acts as a beacon, challenge, stimulus to reflection and rebuke when practice falls short. The ideal type makes commitments explicit: multiperspectival exploration, appropriate multimethodology, flexible intervention and balanced evaluation. It should not be treated as a checklist whose literal completion guarantees success. Its value is normative and developmental—it helps practitioners see what is missing and improve their judgement about what is realistically achievable.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an actual intervention with the ideal type and identify justified adaptations and unjustified omissions."
Key point:
- An ideal type guides reflection without pretending to be fully replicable.
- Use it to improve judgement, not to claim procedural perfection.
30. Q: Why is CSP non-linear in practice?
A: Show answer
Although CSP is presented in four stages, complex situations change as interventions unfold. New evidence, stakeholder reactions, external shocks and unintended effects can alter the problem framing, priorities and suitable methods. Consequently, participants move back and forth among Explore, Produce, Intervene and Check. Check creates moments to pause and reflect, but its outcomes feed into a new cycle of Explore. Non-linearity is therefore a response to the nature of the system, not an excuse for disorder. Teams still need traceability, objectives and review points.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a feedback loop showing how Check can trigger a new Explore stage."
Key point:
- Non-linearity reflects changing systems and learning.
- CSP cycles rather than progresses once from diagnosis to closure.
31. Q: How does CSP address VUCA conditions?
A: Show answer
CSP addresses volatility, uncertainty, complexity and ambiguity by avoiding premature certainty, using multiple perspectives, combining methods and keeping the intervention adaptable. Volatility encourages frequent review; uncertainty calls for learning rather than overconfident prediction; complexity requires attention to interactions and boundaries; ambiguity requires dialogue about meaning and purpose. Stakeholder conflict adds another layer because people may disagree about both facts and values. CSP cannot remove VUCA conditions, but it can make them more visible and support more resilient responses.
Study instruction:
- "For a VUCA scenario, identify one CSP response to volatility, uncertainty, complexity and ambiguity."
Key point:
- CSP manages uncertainty through learning and adaptation rather than false precision.
- The method is designed for situations where context shifts during action.
Source links:
-
- Boulding: General Systems Theory—The Skeleton of Science
32. Q: How can stakeholder conflict be handled within CSP?
A: Show answer
CSP handles conflict by making divergent purposes visible, using purposeful and societal/environmental perspectives, and selecting interventions that support understanding, accommodation and attention to power. Conflict is not treated merely as noise to be eliminated; it can reveal genuine differences in values, distribution and system boundaries. The team must distinguish disagreements about facts from disagreements about what should count as improvement. Dialogue should be supported by evidence, transparent decision rules and inclusion of affected but less powerful stakeholders. Agreement is desirable but should not be manufactured by suppressing dissent.
Study instruction:
- "Map a stakeholder conflict into factual, value-based and power-based dimensions."
Key point:
- Conflict can be diagnostic information about purposes and power.
- A legitimate intervention must make disagreement discussable, not invisible.
33. Q: How does CSP relate to reflective pragmatism?
A: Show answer
Reflective pragmatism treats social inquiry as future-forming and world-making rather than merely mirroring an objective reality. It asks what research contributes to the world, who benefits, who may be harmed and who is absent. This is close to CSP's concern with practical improvement, values, consequences and multiperspectival understanding. Both approaches reject the idea that analysis is politically neutral. CSP adds an explicit systems framework for combining perspectives and methods, while reflective pragmatism emphasises social utility and ethical scrutiny of outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the questions “useful for whom?”, “who may be harmed?” and “who is absent?” to an intervention proposal."
Key point:
- Practical usefulness must be examined together with power and consequences.
- Inquiry is not neutral when it shapes future possibilities.
34. Q: How does CSP relate to phronetic social science?
A: Show answer
Phronetic social science draws on Aristotle's idea of phronesis: practical wisdom that combines understanding and virtue to produce right action in particular circumstances. It asks what actors are doing, whether it is desirable, who gains or loses, what power mechanisms operate and what should be done. CSP is complementary because it also aims at values-directed change, reflects on how models frame reality and pays attention to power. Phronesis strengthens CSP's emphasis on context-sensitive judgement; CSP contributes a structured multiperspectival and multimethodological practice.
Study instruction:
- "Use the phronetic questions to evaluate a proposed reform before selecting methods."
Key point:
- Good systems practice requires practical wisdom, not only technical knowledge.
- Context and power matter when deciding what action is right.
35. Q: What is relational systems thinking, and how does it differ from conventional objectivist analysis?
A: Show answer
Relational systems thinking views knowledge as situated and relational. It attends to interconnections between people, non-humans, future generations and Mother Earth, and recognises that the observer's location and relationships matter. Conventional objectivist analysis often seeks to exclude the observer to secure neutrality. Relational systems thinking instead asks how multiple ways of knowing can be incorporated respectfully into awareness-based systems change. CSP shares the concern with multiple perspectives but must be re-theorised carefully when used across cultural traditions.
Study instruction:
- "Identify whose knowledge is treated as authoritative in a project and what relational knowledge is missing."
Key point:
- Knowledge is situated in relationships and responsibilities.
- Respectful plurality requires more than adding another data source.
36. Q: What is the two-row wampum metaphor in relational systems thinking?
A: Show answer
The two rows symbolise the Mohawk canoe and the Dutch sailing ship travelling alongside one another. Each represents a valuable experiential knowledge system that can learn from the other without one dominating the other. The metaphor supports “two-eyed seeing”: using more than one worldview to expose blind spots and choose an appropriate lens or harmonisation for the circumstances. Equality and friendship are possible because both travel the river of life together. In systems practice, the metaphor cautions against treating one worldview as the universal standard for all situations.
Study instruction:
- "Use two-row thinking to compare an Indigenous and a Western framing of the same system problem."
Key point:
- Plurality should enable mutual learning, not assimilation.
- Different knowledge systems can coexist without one being absorbed by the other.
37. Q: What is Indigenous trans-systemics?
A: Show answer
Indigenous trans-systemics is presented as an approach that turns up the volume of Indigenous perspectives in systems thinking and expands the voices heard in studying, engaging with and transforming systems. In the Anishinabe worldview described in the chapter, everything is connected, relationships are central and individuals are embedded in systems for which they take responsibility. Trans-systemics calls for humility, acceptance of different ways of knowing and critical reflection on the limitations of every perspective. It also challenges the ability of older Western critical-systems sources to address multiple overlapping systems and worldviews.
Study instruction:
- "Review a systems project and identify where Indigenous responsibility, relationality or worldview is being excluded."
Key point:
- Trans-systemics expands the voices and responsibilities considered in systems practice.
- Critical reflection must include limits of the dominant tradition.
38. Q: What is WSR (wuli-shili-renli)?
A: Show answer
WSR is a Chinese multimethodology that examines real-world projects through three interacting domains: wuli, relations within the world or objective existence; shili, relations between the self and the world, including subjective ways of seeing and doing; and renli, intersubjective human relations and caring. The approach encourages practitioners to investigate and model objective conditions, reflect on situated ways of knowing and acting, and manage human relationships. Its similarity to CSP lies in its holistic consideration of multiple perspectives and methods, while its conceptual roots and vocabulary come from Chinese pragmatist philosophy and practice.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the three WSR domains to a technology implementation project."
Key point:
- WSR combines objective investigation, situated sense-making and human care.
- Holistic practice must include facts, experience and relationships.
39. Q: How can CSP avoid methodological imperialism when used across cultures?
A: Show answer
CSP can avoid methodological imperialism by treating its Western origins as historically situated rather than universal, inviting alternative systems traditions into the inquiry, including affected knowledge holders, making power relations explicit and allowing the framing of the situation to be revised. The chapter emphasises the possibility of peaceful coexistence between Indigenous and non-Indigenous ways of thinking and the need for respectful translation. Practitioners should not simply rename local knowledge in Western categories or use a token consultation to legitimise a predetermined method. The test is whether the intervention expands agency, understanding and responsibility for those whose systems are being changed.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a project for tokenism, translation bias and exclusion of local knowledge."
Key point:
- Cross-cultural practice requires humility and shared authority.
- Respectful pluralism changes how the problem is framed, not only who is consulted.
40. Q: What are the main risks of using CSP poorly?
A: Show answer
Poor use of CSP can create methodological confusion, excessive consultation, paralysis, superficial pluralism, hidden power effects, or a false impression that every perspective has been adequately addressed. Practitioners may list five perspectives without allowing them to change the intervention, select methods by fashion rather than fit, overload participants with parallel methodologies, or evaluate only convenient outcomes. Another risk is treating the EPIC framework as linear and rigid, which defeats its adaptive purpose. Quality depends on disciplined selection, transparent reasoning, inclusion, review and willingness to revisit assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Identify five failure modes that could occur in a badly designed CSP intervention and propose controls."
Key point:
- Plurality without disciplined judgement can become noise.
- CSP requires method fit, inclusion, traceability and reflective evaluation.
41. Q: How should success measures be designed in the Produce stage?
A: Show answer
Success measures should reflect the objectives of the intervention and be connected to the concerns raised by the five systemic perspectives. They should include appropriate indicators of efficiency and efficacy, interaction effects and unintended consequences, viability and resilience, effectiveness and stakeholder accommodation, and distributional and environmental outcomes where relevant. Measures should be feasible to collect, understood by participants and reviewed as the situation changes. A single composite indicator may hide trade-offs. The point is not to quantify everything, but to make the intended improvement and possible harms discussable.
Study instruction:
- "Create a balanced success-measure set for a public-service intervention and identify data sources."
Key point:
- Measures should reflect both intended outcomes and possible side effects.
- A narrow metric can reward local optimisation at the expense of system improvement.
42. Q: How should decision-makers agree the next steps after Check?
A: Show answer
Next steps should be based on the balanced evaluation, the learning gained and the priorities of decision-makers and affected stakeholders. Options may include continuing the current intervention, adapting methods, addressing secondary issues, scaling, pausing, reversing a harmful change or beginning a new CSP cycle. Agreement should specify responsibilities, time horizons, review points and what evidence would trigger reconsideration. The next step is not simply “close the project”; CSP assumes that complex improvement is ongoing and that learning must feed forward into future action.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a next-step decision record that includes options, evidence, owners and review triggers."
Key point:
- Check should lead to an explicit, evidence-informed next cycle or closure decision.
- Continuous improvement requires decisions, not reflection alone.
43. Q: How can managers use CSP without formal systems expertise?
A: Show answer
CSP can be used by decision-makers, managers, consultants and people without formal power because the framework provides practical prompts rather than requiring mastery of every systems tradition. Managers can convene stakeholders, frame the situation broadly, ask questions from five perspectives, identify primary and secondary issues, select specialist support where needed, and create review points. The essential discipline is to remain open to competing explanations, make assumptions explicit and ensure that those affected can influence the inquiry. Formal expertise helps, but the chapter presents CSP as a practice that develops participants' systems-thinking capability through use.
Study instruction:
- "Design a simple CSP workshop agenda for a cross-functional management team."
Key point:
- Managers need disciplined questions and inclusion more than technical jargon.
- CSP can build capability while addressing a live problem.
44. Q: How can CSP support continuous improvement?
A: Show answer
CSP supports continuous improvement by linking exploration, intervention, evaluation and learning in repeated cycles. It does not treat improvement as a one-time project with a permanent endpoint. Each cycle can reveal new priorities, unintended consequences, methodological lessons and opportunities for redesign. Continuous improvement is balanced rather than purely efficiency-driven: the aim is improvement viewed across all five perspectives. This may mean accepting slower progress in one dimension to protect viability, fairness or sustainability in another.
Study instruction:
- "Design a quarterly review cycle that uses EPIC to reassess a changing programme."
Key point:
- Continuous improvement is iterative, plural and attentive to consequences.
- Improvement means better system performance and better understanding of the system.
45. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 4?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is that complex situations cannot be responsibly improved through a single, supposedly universal method. CSP offers EPIC as an ideal-type practice: Explore a situation through five systemic perspectives, Produce a coherent intervention strategy using appropriate methodologies and methods, Intervene flexibly as the situation changes, and Check improvements, learning and next steps. The approach is non-linear, multiperspectival, multimethodological and sensitive to power, culture, purpose, viability and the environment. Its wider significance is ethical as well as technical: good systems practice asks not only whether an intervention works, but for whom, at what cost, under what assumptions and with what consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Summarise the chapter in four sentences, one for each EPIC stage, and add one sentence on plural knowledge."
Key point:
- Complex improvement requires plural perspectives, adaptive action and reflective judgement.
- The best solution is not the neatest one; it is the one that remains responsible in context.
Source links:
-
- Boulding: General Systems Theory—The Skeleton of Science
Rows: 45
Table 5
Explore: Five Systemic Perspectives for Understanding Complex Situations
1. Q: What is the Explore stage of Critical Systems Practice (CSP), and what is its main purpose?
A: Show answer
Explore is the first stage of Critical Systems Practice. Its purpose is to develop a rich appreciation of a situation of interest before practitioners decide how to intervene. It does this by examining the situation through five systemic perspectives: mechanical, interrelationships, organismic, purposeful, and societal/environmental. Each perspective draws on established ideas from philosophy, social science, organisation theory and systems thinking, and each highlights issues that may be overlooked by the others. Explore does not itself prescribe a detailed solution. It helps participants identify the primary and secondary issues that should guide the later stages of CSP.
Study instruction:
- "Explain where Explore sits in the EPIC cycle and describe the kind of understanding it should produce before intervention methods are selected."
Key point:
- Explore creates a broad, structured understanding of the situation of interest.
- Its output is an informed view of primary and secondary issues, not a finished solution.
2. Q: Why does Explore use multiple systemic perspectives rather than one preferred way of seeing a problem?
A: Show answer
Complex situations contain technical, relational, organisational, cultural, political, human and environmental dimensions. A single perspective makes some features prominent while suppressing others. Explore therefore uses multiple systemic perspectives to increase the range and flexibility of inquiry. This is consistent with the chapter's general-complexity orientation: decision-makers cannot expect one model or theory to capture the whole situation, but they can use several partial views to develop a more useful appreciation. Differences between perspectives may produce conflicting explanations, yet those conflicts can reveal assumptions and issues that would otherwise remain hidden.
Study instruction:
- "Take a complex organisational issue and identify at least three dimensions that a single technical or managerial perspective might overlook."
Key point:
- Multiple perspectives broaden the initial understanding of a complex situation.
- Conflicting insights can be useful evidence rather than a reason to discard one perspective.
3. Q: What does a general-complexity orientation imply for the way practitioners begin an Explore inquiry?
A: Show answer
A general-complexity orientation rejects the expectation that complex social situations can be fully understood through one universal model or simplified into a complete set of predictable relationships. Practitioners must accept that knowledge is partial, interpretations differ and interventions may have unforeseen effects.
