- Draw a causal chain showing how an intervention could increase the importance of a previously secondary issue.
Key point:
- Issue priority is dynamic.
- Progress on one objective can create risks for another through system feedback.
11. Q: What does it mean to revisit Produce during Intervene?
A: Show answer
Revisiting Produce means reconsidering the intervention strategy when renewed exploration shows that a significant reorientation is needed. The team may change the leading methodology, combine approaches differently, introduce another model or method, or alter the sequence of actions. This is not a change made merely for convenience. It is a deliberate response to updated understanding, with an explanation of what has changed, why the previous approach is insufficient, and how the revised strategy better addresses the situation.
Study instruction:
- Write a short proposal linking new findings from Explore to a revised intervention strategy.
Key point:
- Produce is revisited when new understanding requires a strategic adjustment.
- A revised choice should be justified by the issues and evidence now present.
12. Q: How do Explore–Produce feedback loops protect methodological diversity?
A: Show answer
Repeated returns to Explore reveal whether new concerns, stakeholders, or consequences require attention. Returning to Produce then allows the team to select or combine approaches suited to those developments. This prevents the initial methodology from becoming a permanent boundary around the intervention. The chapter describes regular, rapid iteration as a sensible way to protect methodological diversity. The value is not constant switching; it is preserving the ability to switch when the situation provides a sound reason.
Study instruction:
- Map how new stakeholder evidence could lead from renewed Explore to a revised Produce decision.
Key point:
- Feedback loops preserve the ability to use different approaches.
- Diversity is protected through deliberate review, not indiscriminate switching.
13. Q: What is the difference between flexible adaptation and inconsistent practice?
A: Show answer
Flexible adaptation changes an intervention in response to evidence while preserving a clear purpose and an explainable rationale. Inconsistent practice changes methods without understanding why, loses sight of the primary issues, or creates confusion about responsibilities. A disciplined practitioner records the original rationale, monitors whether assumptions remain valid, and explains why a different approach is now more suitable. Coherence comes from the reasoning that connects each adjustment to the evolving situation, not from using one method forever.
Study instruction:
- Compare a justified methodological change with an arbitrary change and identify the evidence supporting the first.
Key point:
- Flexibility is purposeful and evidence-responsive.
- Consistency depends on a clear rationale, not permanent adherence to one method.
14. Q: How can practitioners avoid closing off important possibilities during Intervene?
A: Show answer
Practitioners should maintain access to different systemic perspectives, invite information from affected stakeholders, and check whether the intervention still reflects the situation's complexity. They should ask which voices are missing, which assumptions have become questionable, and what the current methodology cannot address. The chapter links this openness to frequent returns to Explore and Produce. This keeps alternatives available while still requiring reasoned decisions about which actions are appropriate.
Study instruction:
- At a project review, ask whose perspective is missing and which assumptions have not been tested.
Key point:
- Premature closure excludes relevant perspectives or alternatives.
- Review and stakeholder engagement keep the intervention open to learning.
15. Q: What should an intervention team monitor while action is underway?
A: Show answer
The team should monitor progress against the agreed purpose and changes in the wider situation. Relevant matters include whether primary issues are being addressed, whether stakeholders remain engaged, whether methods produce useful learning, whether unintended effects are emerging, and whether contextual constraints have changed. The chapter's examples show that the most important development may be a new ethical or social issue rather than a simple delivery variance. Monitoring should therefore combine implementation information with continuing inquiry into what improvement means for those affected.
Study instruction:
- Design a review agenda combining delivery progress with stakeholder, contextual, ethical, and unintended-effect questions.
Key point:
- Monitor both implementation and changes in the situation.
- Activity measures alone may not show whether the intervention remains appropriate.
16. Q: How should methodological flexibility be balanced with situational constraints?
A: Show answer
CSP's pluralist intent must be pursued while respecting practical limits such as time, resources, authority, expertise, and participants' capacity to engage. A theoretically attractive approach may be impractical if the organization cannot support it. However, constraints should not automatically justify excluding marginalized voices or ignoring serious ethical issues. The intervention should make limitations explicit and choose a workable combination of methods without pretending that it addresses every concern. The chapter points to time and cost as important constraints for further consideration.
Study instruction:
- Identify the main constraints in a proposed intervention and explain how its design can adapt without losing its purpose.
Key point:
- Pluralist ambition must be translated into context-sensitive practice.
- Constraints should be acknowledged rather than used to conceal exclusions.
17. Q: Why should local experience be privileged during Intervene?
A: Show answer
People working within a situation often understand its history, culture, informal practices, relationships, and politics in ways an external expert cannot immediately reproduce. The chapter warns against systems practice that claims to replace local knowledge with technical expertise or forces people into a single inflexible methodology. Practitioners should work with decision-makers and affected people, using systemic tools to support insight rather than override situated experience. Local knowledge is not beyond question, but it is essential evidence for designing changes that can work in context.
Study instruction:
- Identify what local participants know that an external project team might overlook and show how it should influence action.
Key point:
- Local experience is a core input to context-sensitive intervention.
- Systems expertise should support, not displace, the knowledge of people in the situation.
18. Q: Why is Intervene not intended to be an expert-led procedure followed rigidly?
A: Show answer
A formal description can make Intervene appear to be a technical sequence controlled by specialists. The chapter cautions against this interpretation. CSP aims to help people think insightfully about complexity and improve their situations, not impose a formula that replaces their judgment. Practitioners contribute perspectives, models, and methods, but work with decision-makers and other participants to shape action. This collaborative role reduces the risk of ignoring local knowledge or forcing a problem into an unsuitable template.
Study instruction:
- Explain how an external practitioner can contribute expertise while keeping decision-makers and affected people actively involved.
Key point:
- CSP is collaborative rather than a rigid expert prescription.
- Practitioners support local decision-making with systemic insight and suitable methods.
19. Q: How did the Hull University Business School example use an engineering perspective?
A: Show answer
The school needed a clear goal and resources to pursue it, with accreditation from three major business-school accrediting bodies as an initial objective. An engineering-oriented approach helped organize processes and resources around that goal. Student numbers needed to grow, so recruitment processes were streamlined; larger cohorts were managed through a common undergraduate first year, shared postgraduate modules, and a staff workload model. The example shows how an engineering perspective can improve processes and capacity, while remaining only one component of a broader intervention.
Study instruction:
- Identify the goal, resource problem, and process changes in the Hull example.
Key point:
- Engineering-oriented action aligned processes and resources with a defined objective.
- Operational efficiency alone could not address every organizational issue.
20. Q: How did the Viable System Model contribute to the Hull intervention?
A: Show answer
The Viable System Model supported the organismic perspective by helping the school identify its primary activities and organize itself around them. It focused attention on managerial support and the autonomy needed for those activities to function. The school also needed to remain viable as part of its host university, leading to dedicated marketing, alumni, administration, finance, human-resource, and quality functions. The chapter notes that arrangements changed as the school and its environment co-evolved, so the VSM acted as a guide rather than a permanent organizational chart.
Study instruction:
- Explain how the VSM informed primary activities, autonomy, and support functions in the Hull case.
Key point:
- The VSM focused attention on viability, autonomy, and support for primary activities.
- Organizational arrangements must evolve with the environment.
21. Q: What is the significance of autonomy within a host organization in the Hull example?
A: Show answer
The business school needed enough autonomy to respond to its competitive and academic environment while remaining part of the wider university. The VSM helped examine how the school could secure decision-making capacity without losing its connection to the host system. Autonomy did not mean isolation: the school still depended on wider governance, resources, and accountability. The example shows that the right balance depends on the unit's work and environment, and may need adjustment as both change.
Study instruction:
- Diagram the relationship between an organizational unit's autonomy and its dependence on a larger host system.
Key point:
- Viability may require meaningful autonomy within a wider organization.
- Autonomy must be balanced with integration and accountability.
22. Q: How did the interrelationships perspective reveal an unintended consequence in the Hull case?
A: Show answer
Recruiting high-performing researchers could improve research performance but risk neglecting teaching. Poor teaching results could affect student surveys, league-table position, applications, student numbers, income, and ultimately the school's capacity to sustain research. This chain shows how a narrow improvement can undermine wider viability through feedback. The chapter argues for a balancing loop that reinforces good teaching practice. Interrelationships analysis therefore helps identify consequences that are not visible when each objective is considered separately.
Study instruction:
- Draw the causal sequence from research recruitment to possible decline in teaching, student numbers, income, and research capacity.
Key point:
- A local improvement can create system-wide consequences.
- Feedback analysis reveals the need to balance competing objectives.
23. Q: What did the purposeful systemic perspective contribute to the Hull intervention?
A: Show answer
The purposeful perspective emphasized shared understanding and direction. Formal and informal forums, frequent strategic and operational meetings, and a vision of responsible leadership for a complex world helped focus collective effort. Strategic Assumption Surfacing and Testing was used to examine assumptions about stakeholders. Elements of Soft Systems Methodology helped review the situation, develop possible ways forward, and agree changes that were feasible and desirable. These practices connected different viewpoints to coordinated action.
Study instruction:
- Identify the dialogue mechanisms and strategic tools used in Hull and explain how they supported shared purpose.
Key point:
- Purposeful approaches create forums for mutual understanding and direction.
- Vision and assumption testing help connect discussion to action.
24. Q: How were SAST and Soft Systems Methodology used in the Hull case?
A: Show answer
Strategic Assumption Surfacing and Testing helped the school examine beliefs about stakeholders that influenced strategic decisions. Soft Systems Methodology informed practices of reviewing the situation, considering possible ways forward, and agreeing feasible and desirable changes. At times it structured debate more explicitly, including through a Rich Picture of the school's strategic position. The example shows that methods can be used selectively within a wider intervention rather than requiring an organization to follow one methodology in full.
Study instruction:
- Identify one strategic assumption to test and one way a Rich Picture could structure discussion.
Key point:
- SAST makes strategic assumptions visible and open to challenge.
- SSM can structure learning and debate about feasible and desirable change.
25. Q: Why did the societal and environmental perspective matter in the Hull intervention?
A: Show answer
This perspective widened attention beyond internal performance. It brought issues such as women's representation in senior posts, minority ethnic and LGBT+ concerns, and the academic-administrative divide into view. It also supported concessions for disadvantaged local students, fair-trade coffee, responsible-management education, business ethics teaching, and sustainable-business content. The intervention was therefore not judged solely by accreditation or financial performance; social inclusion, ethical responsibility, and environmental concerns were also treated as dimensions of improvement.
Study instruction:
- List the social and environmental issues in Hull and explain how they broaden the meaning of improvement.
Key point:
- Improvement includes social, ethical, and environmental dimensions.
- A societal perspective reveals who benefits, who is excluded, and what wider effects matter.
26. Q: What does it mean that all five systemic perspectives remained alive in the Hull intervention?
A: Show answer
The perspectives were used in combination, with different ones becoming prominent, receding, and resurfacing as the intervention developed. They did not receive equal attention at every moment. Engineering concerns supported goals and processes; organismic concerns supported viability; interrelationships analysis revealed feedback; purposeful approaches developed shared direction; and societal or environmental concerns widened attention to inclusion and sustainability. The example illustrates pluralism as an ongoing capability rather than a checklist completed once.
Study instruction:
- Create a timeline showing how different perspectives could become more or less prominent during an intervention.
Key point:
- Pluralism retains access to multiple perspectives over time.
- The prominence of a perspective can change without making others irrelevant.
27. Q: Why was Critical Systems Heuristics selected in the UTSAH child-protection example?
A: Show answer
UTSAH, an Indian child-protection NGO, sought to translate government policy into local action against violence, exploitation, and abuse. Initial exploration showed that many stakeholders needed to work together and differed in beliefs, assumptions about participation, and power. Critical Systems Heuristics was selected because it could reveal viewpoints, expose tensions, and give less powerful stakeholders a voice. The choice reflected the social and political nature of the situation, not simply a technical need to design a programme.
Study instruction:
- Explain why stakeholder diversity and unequal power made CSH a suitable starting approach for UTSAH.
Key point:
- CSH was chosen to surface viewpoints, tensions, and power relations.
- Method selection should reflect the social and political character of the problem.
28. Q: How did boundary questions support the UTSAH intervention?
A: Show answer
The twelve CSH boundary questions structured interviews with UTSAH staff and external stakeholders. They helped make explicit who was included in defining the problem, who benefited, who held authority, what knowledge counted, and whose interests might be overlooked. This mattered because child-protection work involved actors with different beliefs and unequal influence. Insights from the interviews were consolidated and taken into a convergence workshop, providing a basis for prioritizing programme areas and designing a roadmap.
Study instruction:
- Identify the kinds of inclusion, authority, and beneficiary questions a boundary review should raise in a multi-stakeholder project.
Key point:
- Boundary questions expose assumptions about inclusion and decision authority.
- Structured interviews can reveal tensions that a technical plan may miss.
29. Q: Why did UTSAH add CATWOE and an issue-rating matrix after the initial CSH work?
A: Show answer
CSH helped stakeholders articulate different viewpoints and develop mutual understanding. At the convergence workshop, the team recognized that further methods were needed to prioritize programme areas and design a future roadmap. CATWOE, associated with Soft Systems Methodology, and an adapted issue-rating matrix supplied a more action-oriented focus. The change did not mean CSH had failed; it had helped create the conditions for a different task. The example shows how methods can evolve as the intervention moves from understanding toward planning.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the roles of CSH, CATWOE, and an issue-rating matrix in the UTSAH sequence.
Key point:
- A successful initial method can create the conditions for another method to become useful.
- Method combinations evolve as intervention tasks change.
30. Q: What does UTSAH show about moving from emancipatory inquiry to goal-directed planning?
A: Show answer
The project began by making power relations and different viewpoints visible. Once those insights were consolidated and discussed, CATWOE and an issue-rating matrix helped participants specify and prioritize a roadmap. The transition did not mean that participation was no longer important. The chapter emphasizes continuing to revisit Explore to check whether alignment of purposes remains justifiable. The case shows that a participative approach can lead to more goal-directed work when sufficient understanding has developed, but apparent agreement should not be treated as permanent consensus.
Study instruction:
- Explain how participative inquiry can transition into structured planning without losing stakeholder legitimacy.
Key point:
- Different stages may require different methodological emphases.
- A move toward action must preserve attention to whether stakeholder purposes remain aligned.
31. Q: What lesson does the National Health Service example provide about repeated problem structuring?
A: Show answer
The NHS intervention used the VSM, SSM, and SAST sequentially. It illustrated the importance of returning continually to problem structuring as the intervention proceeded. The team did not treat its initial understanding as final; it used renewed exploration to reorient problem-solving when necessary. The chapter's broader point is that problem structuring must flow through an intervention as an iterative activity rather than being completed once before implementation begins.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a complex health-system intervention may need repeated problem-structuring reviews.
Key point:
- Problem structuring continues throughout intervention.
- Repeated review keeps problem-solving aligned with the changing situation.
32. Q: How did Kingston Gas Turbines demonstrate responsiveness to changing circumstances?
A: Show answer
The three-year engagement supported an ambitious strategy to double the business. Changing business and environmental circumstances required frequent changes in the dominant systems methodology and the use of many models, methods, tools, and techniques. Systems-derived tools such as Rich Pictures, Root Definitions, Purposeful Activity Models, the VSM, and System Dynamics were combined with methods from outside the systems tradition. The example shows why a long-running strategic intervention needs a broad repertoire rather than one fixed approach.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a multi-year growth strategy may require methodological changes as conditions evolve.
Key point:
- Long interventions are exposed to changing circumstances.
- A broad repertoire can support adaptation when dominant issues shift.
33. Q: How were SAST and the VSM used in the Hull and East Yorkshire Children's University example?
A: Show answer
As the charity grew, SAST was used to challenge and rethink its strategy in light of new opportunities. When it took on additional tasks, especially work with children in care, and expanded geographically into East Yorkshire, the VSM helped guide two restructuring exercises. These sought a better match between the organization's variety and the variety of its environment. The example distinguishes strategic assumption testing from organizational design: each method supported a different need arising as the charity developed.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the strategic role of SAST from the organizational-design role of the VSM in a growing charity.
Key point:
- SAST supported strategic reconsideration as opportunities changed.
- The VSM informed structure as activities and the environment expanded.
34. Q: How did the older people's housing project show the importance of changing problem boundaries?
A: Show answer
The project initially aimed to improve how information from assessments could be aggregated for housing policy. Interviews revealed that this remit was too narrow: older people described mismatches between what they requested and what was provided, along with problems in multi-agency working. With sponsors' agreement, the remit and boundaries were expanded. Workshops then explored the desired properties of an ideal housing system using Idealized Design and CSH questions. The case shows how stakeholder evidence can reveal that an initial problem definition excludes important concerns.
Study instruction:
- Identify evidence that a project's original boundary is too narrow and explain how a revised scope could be agreed.
Key point:
- Stakeholder evidence can reveal missing dimensions of a problem.
- Revised boundaries may be needed for a more coherent multi-agency response.
35. Q: Why did the housing project combine Idealized Design, CSH, and the VSM?
A: Show answer
Idealized Design and CSH questions helped participants describe the properties they wanted in an ideal housing system and consider whose interests were represented. Agreement across workshops gave managers a basis for designing improved multi-agency working. The VSM then guided consideration of how agencies could coordinate without relying solely on hierarchy and what information flows were needed. The combination moved from exploring desirable futures and boundary concerns toward organizational design, with each method contributing to a distinct task.
Study instruction:
- Map the progression from stakeholder exploration to an agreed ideal and then to coordination design.
Key point:
- Methods can be sequenced according to the tasks an intervention must perform.
- The VSM informed coordination after the desired properties had been explored.
36. Q: What ethical issue changed the focus of the Niger Delta food-production intervention?
A: Show answer
The company initially wanted to use lean thinking to minimize waste and consider reuse. A wider societal and environmental perspective revealed that local residents were affected by waste, including livestock waste dumped near where they lived. Community members described a severe stench and a potential public-health hazard. The issue therefore extended beyond operational efficiency to the effects of company activity on people outside its immediate boundaries. The company was willing to engage with these concerns, allowing the intervention to broaden its focus.
Study instruction:
- Explain how an efficient waste project can be ethically incomplete when affected community stakeholders are excluded.
Key point:
- The case exposed social and environmental effects missed by a narrow efficiency focus.
- Stakeholder engagement can change what an intervention must address.
37. Q: How did CSH and other methods broaden the Niger Delta inquiry?
A: Show answer
The project used a CSH logic to interview community members and the company. Workshops then employed Rich Pictures, CATWOE, and Purposeful Activity Models to reveal complexity and express the perspectives relevant to the situation. These methods moved the intervention beyond a narrow technical definition of waste management. The company could see that broader engagement complemented the original lean approach. The example shows how methods can bring neglected interests into view and support a more comprehensive understanding.
Study instruction:
- Describe how interviews and workshop methods can include external stakeholders in a technical improvement project.
Key point:
- CSH helped surface community and company perspectives.
- Rich Pictures, CATWOE, and Purposeful Activity Models supported a broader inquiry.
38. Q: What ethical responsibility does the Niger Delta example suggest for systems practitioners?
A: Show answer
Practitioners must consider whether an organization is willing to recognize and respond to the effects of its activities on wider society. In the Niger Delta case, the company was willing to listen to community concerns. The chapter acknowledges that this will not always happen: if an organization refuses to change harmful practices, a practitioner may need a stronger emancipatory stance or reconsider whether continued assistance is desirable. The reference to Churchman's challenge about helping an organization commit suicide emphasizes that technical competence does not remove ethical responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Identify ethical warning signs that would cause a practitioner to question continued involvement in an intervention.
Key point:
- Practitioners must assess whether their work enables harmful practices.
- Ethical responsibility may require a stronger stance or withdrawal.
39. Q: How did the green-innovation project change when metal recovery appeared commercially unviable?
A: Show answer
The project concerned recovering valuable metals from steel slag at a former steelworks. The initial plan was to hold a workshop about the technology, but it became clear that rare-metal recovery was unlikely to be commercially viable at that site. The workshop was reframed as a community-based event exploring possible future uses for the whole site. This reflected the realization that the technology question could not be separated from the site's wider strategic future. New evidence about viability justified a broader intervention.
Study instruction:
- Explain why evidence that a technology is not commercially viable may require reconsidering a project's wider purpose.
Key point:
- New evidence about viability can change intervention focus.
- The project moved from a narrow technology question to the future of the whole site.
40. Q: Why did the green-innovation example consider social desirability alongside technical and economic factors?
A: Show answer
The revised workshop considered possible uses for the whole site, not only metal recovery. This required attention to whether an option was technically feasible, economically viable, and socially desirable. A technically workable and commercially attractive proposal may still be inappropriate if it fails to address the needs and values of the affected community. The example shows that improvement cannot be reduced to technical performance or financial return. Social desirability is a substantive consideration when deciding which future is worth pursuing.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a project option against technical feasibility, economic viability, and social desirability.
Key point:
- Technical feasibility and economic viability are not sufficient by themselves.
- Social desirability connects intervention choices to affected people and places.
41. Q: How did the North Yorkshire Police example show the need to change methodology to prevent failure?
A: Show answer
The intervention initially used an organismic orientation and the VSM to encourage greater local autonomy for divisions. When the project faced imminent failure, the team shifted toward a purposeful perspective and SSM. It became clear that the force lacked a cultural appreciation of how decentralization could work. Changing perceptions and building understanding therefore needed priority over continuing with the original structural emphasis. The case shows that a methodology may be conceptually suitable but practically ineffective if organizational culture is not ready to support the proposed change.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish a structural design problem from a cultural-understanding problem in the police example.
Key point:
- A threatened intervention may need a different methodological emphasis.
- Cultural understanding can be a prerequisite for structural change.
42. Q: What did the Manchester project involving young people living on the streets reveal about participation?
A: Show answer
The project investigated the circumstances of young people under sixteen living on the streets and sought suitable responses. It was difficult to involve vulnerable young people and create the space and encouragement needed for them to express their views. Agencies also held conflicting viewpoints, and some might feel threatened by what emerged. The project's survival and useful outcomes depended on continued reflection about marginalization, power, and participation. The example shows that meaningful participation requires attention to the conditions under which people can speak.
Study instruction:
- Assess whether a participation plan gives vulnerable people safety, time, support, and influence.
Key point:
- Participation is affected by power and practical conditions for speaking.
- Flexibility must include reflection on marginalization and institutional interests.
43. Q: What broad reasons for switching methodology are identified in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter groups switches into three broad triggers. A change may occur through the natural progression of an intervention as the work moves to a new task; it may be provoked by a significant event that changes the focus; or drastic action may be needed to save a project from collapse. These triggers differ in urgency, but each can justify reconsidering the leading methodology or combination of methods. Methodological change is therefore not necessarily evidence of poor planning; it can be a response to learning or changed circumstances.
Study instruction:
- Classify project developments as natural progression, significant event, or project-rescue trigger.
Key point:
- Method changes can follow normal progression, new events, or a threat to project viability.
- The reason for switching should be explicit.
44. Q: How can a practitioner determine whether a methodology switch is necessary?
A: Show answer
A switch is justified when renewed exploration shows that the current approach no longer addresses the primary issues, excludes important perspectives, or cannot respond to material change. Evidence may include disengaged stakeholders, a newly urgent ethical concern, unexpected consequences, failed assumptions, or risk of collapse. Practitioners should compare the current approach's limits with the needs now visible and consider whether adaptation or an added method would be sufficient. The chapter supports reasoned change, not automatic replacement whenever difficulties arise.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a decision note showing evidence, alternatives, and reasons for retaining, adapting, or replacing the current approach.
Key point:
- A switch should respond to a demonstrated mismatch between approach and situation.
- Adaptation or supplementation may be enough; replacement is not always necessary.
45. Q: What is paradigm incommensurability and why does it challenge multimethodology?
A: Show answer
Paradigm incommensurability is the argument that approaches grounded in different paradigms cannot be combined complementarily because their underlying assumptions are fundamentally incompatible. The chapter traces the argument to Kuhn and its extension to organizational analysis by Burrell and Morgan. Critics suggest that combining such approaches would require a practitioner to stand above the paradigms. This objection challenges CSP because it deliberately draws on different systemic perspectives and methodologies. The chapter therefore explains how pluralist practice can be coherent without claiming a superior philosophical standpoint.
Study instruction:
- State the core claim of paradigm incommensurability and explain why it challenges multimethodological practice.
Key point:
- The argument questions whether approaches based on different assumptions can be combined.
- It challenges the theoretical coherence of multimethodological intervention.
46. Q: How did early Critical Systems Thinking risk falling into the metaparadigm problem?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that early Critical Systems Thinking sought to operate as a metaparadigm. That position implies a framework standing above competing paradigms and providing a superior basis for judging or combining them. Such a claim risks reproducing the incommensurability problem because it assumes a privileged standpoint. Discordant pluralism offered another route: reflective conversation between paradigms rather than a claim to stand above them. This shift helped preserve diversity without asserting a universal philosophical vantage point.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a framework claiming to stand above all paradigms may reproduce the problem it seeks to solve.
Key point:
- A metaparadigm claim assumes a privileged position above competing paradigms.
- Discordant pluralism offers dialogue rather than philosophical supremacy.
47. Q: What is discordant pluralism in Critical Systems Thinking?
A: Show answer
Discordant pluralism treats differences among paradigms as a subject for reflective conversation rather than something to erase through a supposedly superior framework. It recognizes that perspectives may rest on conflicting assumptions and keeps those tensions visible. In the chapter, this idea allowed CST to encourage dialogue across paradigms without claiming to occupy a position above them. Its limitation is that it can leave the framework without sufficient theoretical support and open the door to relativism, which motivates the turn to pragmatism.
Study instruction:
- Explain how reflective conversation differs from forcing different perspectives into one unified paradigm.
Key point:
- Discordant pluralism keeps philosophical differences open to critical dialogue.
- It needs a defensible way to evaluate practical choices.
48. Q: Why can discordant pluralism alone open the door to relativism?
A: Show answer
If different perspectives coexist without a basis for evaluating their use, practitioners may struggle to explain why one approach is more suitable than another. The chapter says discordant pluralism avoided the metaparadigm claim but left CST without sufficient theoretical support and opened the door to relativism. Relativism becomes a concern when every approach appears equally acceptable simply because it is different. CSP needs pluralism, but it also needs a reasoned way to judge suitability and consequences in context. Pragmatism supplies that grounding.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish respecting multiple perspectives from treating every intervention as equally justified.
Key point:
- Pluralism does not remove the need for critical evaluation.
- Pragmatism offers a contextual basis for judging approaches.
49. Q: How does pragmatism support flexible multimethodological practice?
A: Show answer
Pragmatism provides a philosophical basis for CST and a way to judge CSP's endeavours. It does not require different systemic perspectives to agree on one account of reality; they can offer different adequate ways of seeing and engaging with the world. Their value can be evaluated by whether they help produce meaningful improvement in the circumstances where they are used. This allows practitioners to combine methodologies without pretending their assumptions are identical, while remaining reflective about purpose, evidence, context, and consequences.
Study instruction:
- Explain how pragmatic evaluation differs from trying to prove that one methodology is universally correct.
Key point:
- Pragmatism evaluates approaches in relation to consequences in context.
- Different methodologies can be combined without claiming identical assumptions.
50. Q: What does the phrase that systemic perspectives must 'pay in life' mean?
A: Show answer
The phrase expresses a pragmatic test: a perspective or methodology should be judged partly by whether it makes a meaningful difference in the situation where it is used. Theoretical elegance is not enough if an approach cannot help people understand or improve their circumstances. Practical usefulness should not mean short-term convenience for the most powerful participant; the chapter's examples show that participation, ethics, social desirability, and wider consequences matter. The phrase connects methodological evaluation to real-world effects while preserving critical judgment.
Study instruction:
- Define meaningful improvement for one proposed method and identify whose experience should count in judging its value.
Key point:
- Pragmatic value is assessed through consequences in the lived situation.
- Usefulness must be considered alongside participation, ethics, and wider effects.
51. Q: How does pragmatism help avoid both incommensurability and relativism?
A: Show answer
Pragmatism does not require methodologies to share one paradigm before they can be used together, so philosophical differences need not prevent combination. It also avoids treating choices as arbitrary: approaches can be evaluated by their adequacy and consequences in the particular situation. The chapter's argument is not that differences disappear, but that practitioners can work across them while remaining explicit about purpose, evidence, context, and the effects of their choices. This provides a practical basis for selection, combination, and revision.
Study instruction:
- Contrast incommensurability and relativism, then show how pragmatic evaluation addresses both.
Key point:
- Pragmatism permits methodological diversity without requiring one shared paradigm.
- It provides a contextual basis for evaluating consequences and adequacy.
52. Q: What is the difference between systemic perspectives and paradigms in the chapter's argument?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that systemic perspectives do not compete to establish one final ontological truth. They offer different, potentially adequate ways of seeing the world and are linked to methodologies for engaging with it. Paradigm incommensurability, by contrast, concerns the difficulty of combining approaches whose underlying assumptions are treated as incompatible. Treating viewpoints as systemic perspectives that can be evaluated pragmatically allows CSP to preserve pluralism without claiming to reconcile every philosophical difference.
Study instruction:
- Explain why framing viewpoints as systemic perspectives changes the practical problem of combining methods.
Key point:
- Systemic perspectives offer different ways of seeing and engaging with a situation.
- Pragmatic adequacy allows contextual judgment without one final worldview.
53. Q: What cognitive, cultural, and societal constraints can affect multimethodological practice?
A: Show answer
The chapter points to cognitive, cultural, and societal constraints, as well as time and cost, for further discussion. Cognitive limits may make complex models difficult to use; cultural expectations may shape who can speak or challenge authority; societal structures may reinforce marginalization or unequal power. The chapter does not fully develop these constraints in this section, so practitioners should treat them as issues requiring further analysis. Flexibility alone cannot remove structural barriers, and an intervention should not promise more than the context can support.
Study instruction:
- List likely cognitive, cultural, and societal barriers in a case and identify which require facilitation or wider structural action.
Key point:
- Multimethodology operates within real social and cognitive constraints.
- Recognizing limits is necessary for realistic intervention design.
54. Q: How should time and cost commitments influence intervention design?
A: Show answer
A pluralist intervention may require several methods, repeated stakeholder engagement, and multiple cycles of Explore and Produce. These activities can increase time and cost, especially when participants have competing responsibilities or need support to participate. The chapter identifies these as constraints but does not develop them in detail here. Practitioners should match the range of methods to the importance and complexity of the issues, making trade-offs explicit. A faster or cheaper approach is not better if it excludes essential perspectives or creates larger downstream failures.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a resource estimate and identify the risks of omitting essential stakeholder or review activities.
Key point:
- Pluralist work has resource implications that should be planned.
- Efficiency should not remove perspectives or safeguards essential to sound intervention.
55. Q: What risks arise when practitioners force a situation into one inflexible methodology?
A: Show answer
A single inflexible approach can narrow the problem definition, overlook stakeholders, and make the intervention insensitive to change. It may privilege the assumptions built into the method and prevent responses to issues requiring another perspective. The chapter's conclusion criticizes systems practice that replaces local knowledge with technical templates or forces problems into a limited range of systems archetypes. A defensible approach treats methodologies as resources for inquiry and action, adapting them when the situation shows that the original fit is inadequate.
Study instruction:
- Identify stakeholders or concerns that could be excluded if a team insists on one fixed methodology.
Key point:
- An inflexible method can distort the situation and marginalize local knowledge.
- Adapt the methodology to the problem rather than forcing the problem to fit it.
56. Q: How can stakeholder participation remain meaningful throughout Intervene?
A: Show answer
Meaningful participation requires more than inviting people to a workshop once. Practitioners should consider who is affected, who has influence, who may be marginalized, and whether participants have safe and practical conditions to express their views. UTSAH, the Manchester project, and the Niger Delta example show why power and access matter. Participation should be revisited as issues change, and people should be told how their contributions influence decisions. New developments may make different groups relevant as the intervention progresses.
Study instruction:
- Review a stakeholder plan for representation, accessibility, safety, influence, feedback, and continued engagement.
Key point:
- Participation must address power and practical barriers to expression.
- Engagement should evolve with the intervention and its affected groups.
57. Q: How should a practitioner respond when an organization refuses to address an ethical concern?
A: Show answer
The practitioner should assess whether continued assistance would enable harmful conduct. In the Niger Delta example, the company was willing to listen to community concerns, but the chapter acknowledges that organizations may refuse to change. In such circumstances, a stronger emancipatory stance may be needed, and the practitioner may need to reconsider whether involvement remains desirable. The reference to Churchman's challenge about helping an organization commit suicide emphasizes that technical competence does not remove responsibility for the consequences of professional work.
Study instruction:
- Develop an escalation and withdrawal framework for persistent refusal to address serious harm.
Key point:
- Practitioners must assess whether their work supports harmful practices.
- Ethical responsibility may require a stronger stance or reconsideration of involvement.
58. Q: How can a team preserve coherence when several methodologies and methods are combined?
A: Show answer
Coherence comes from linking each method to the issues and purpose agreed through Explore and Produce. The team should know what question a method addresses, what assumptions it introduces, what information it generates, and how its outputs inform the next decision. UTSAH combined CSH with CATWOE and an issue-rating matrix for different tasks; Hull used several perspectives for different aspects of improvement. Review points should test whether the combination remains suitable and produces meaningful learning.
Study instruction:
- Create a method map linking each selected approach to an issue, expected output, and subsequent decision.
Key point:
- A multi-method intervention needs an explicit logic connecting tools to issues.
- Purpose and iterative review maintain coherence without methodological uniformity.
59. Q: What should decision-makers ask before approving a major intervention change?
A: Show answer
They should ask what new evidence has emerged, how it changes the understanding of the situation, which stakeholders or issues are newly important, and why the current strategy is insufficient. They should compare adapting an existing method with adding or replacing an approach, then assess implications for participation, resources, timing, and unintended effects. The rationale should explain how the revision remains connected to the intervention's purpose and how its effectiveness will be reviewed. These questions turn flexibility into a transparent governance discipline.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an approval checklist covering evidence, stakeholder impact, alternatives, resources, rationale, and review criteria.
Key point:
- Major changes should be justified by updated understanding and alternatives analysis.
- Decision-makers should assess consequences and define review arrangements.
60. Q: How can a team evaluate whether an intervention is producing improvement rather than merely activity?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should look beyond completed workshops or tasks. Practitioners should examine whether primary issues are being addressed, whether the situation is becoming more workable for affected people, whether understanding has improved, and whether unintended consequences are managed. In Hull, research performance had to be balanced with teaching and wider viability; in the green-innovation case, technical activity was reconsidered when commercial viability was weak. Improvement therefore requires context-sensitive judgment about outcomes, not just completion of planned activities.
Study instruction:
- Define outcome indicators for one intervention and explain how they capture social, organizational, and operational effects.
Key point:
- Activity completion is not the same as meaningful improvement.
- Evaluation should reflect the consequences that matter in the situation.
61. Q: What role does reflection play in flexible multimethodological intervention?
A: Show answer
Reflection allows practitioners and participants to examine whether their understanding, assumptions, methods, and actions remain appropriate. The chapter links reflective conversation to discordant pluralism and repeated returns to Explore and Produce. Reflection helps identify when a method is no longer useful, when an issue has changed, or when a decision has overlooked a stakeholder. A useful review asks what has been learned, which assumptions were challenged, whose interests were affected, what consequences appeared, and what should change next.
Study instruction:
- Conduct an after-action review covering learning, assumptions, stakeholder effects, consequences, and next decisions.
Key point:
- Reflection supports both pluralism and practical adaptation.
- A learning intervention uses evidence from action to revise understanding and strategy.
62. Q: What are the main practical lessons from Chapter 7?
A: Show answer
Intervention should be planned but not rigid. Practitioners begin with methods selected for the primary issues, adapt models and tools to context, and remain ready to revisit Explore and Produce when learning requires a change. The examples show switches caused by normal project progression, new stakeholder or ethical concerns, changed economic assumptions, and threats to project survival. The theoretical discussion supports multimethodology through pluralism and pragmatism rather than a superior metaparadigm. Local knowledge, participation, and ethical responsibility remain central throughout action.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter as a practical intervention cycle and apply it to a complex project.
Key point:
- Flexible intervention depends on pluralism, iteration, and pragmatic evaluation.
- Local knowledge and ethical responsibility remain central throughout action.
Rows: 62
Table 8
Critical Systems Practice: Check and Systemic Evaluation
1. Q: What is the Check stage of Critical Systems Practice (CSP), and what is its purpose?
A: Show answer
Check is the fourth stage of Critical Systems Practice. It asks participants to evaluate the improvements achieved through an intervention, reflect on what they have learned about the situation and the systems approaches used, and discuss and agree on the next steps. Its role is not simply to decide whether a project met its original targets. A CSP intervention is intended to improve a complex situation, so evaluation must consider the benefits and unintended effects experienced by decision-makers and other stakeholders. Check also examines whether the intervention's theories, systemic perspectives and methodologies proved useful. The findings should inform further action, a new CSP cycle, or a reasoned decision to conclude or redirect the work.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the three Check activities in the chapter and explain how each one informs the next."
Key point:
- Check evaluates improvement, captures learning and guides next steps.
- Evaluation is broader than checking whether initial targets were met.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
- American Evaluation Association: What Is Evaluation?
2. Q: Why does CSP treat an intervention as an iterative process rather than a one-off project?
A: Show answer
CSP assumes that complex situations continue to change and that an intervention cannot normally deliver comprehensive, permanent improvement in one pass. New issues can emerge as relationships, priorities, resources and external conditions change. Explore, Produce, Implement and Check therefore inform one another over time rather than forming a rigid sequence that ends once a report is issued. Check can reveal that an earlier assumption was incomplete, that a chosen methodology needs adjustment, or that a new issue deserves attention. A prolonged intervention should repeatedly evaluate its effects and adapt its approach. The chapter also recognises that a particular intervention may stop because priorities change, time or money is constrained, or stakeholders want to assess progress at a particular point.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the EPIC cycle and annotate two examples of feedback from Check that could change a later intervention."
Key point:
- CSP is iterative because complex situations evolve and interventions create new learning.
- A completed project phase does not imply that the wider situation has stopped changing.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
3. Q: Under what circumstances does Check become the central activity in a CSP intervention?
A: Show answer
Check becomes especially important when decision-makers pause or conclude a specific intervention. The trigger may be a change in organisational priorities, a time or budget constraint, or a need to understand what has been achieved so far. It can also become prominent when stakeholders need evidence before deciding whether to continue, redirect, scale, or stop an initiative. The chapter does not present Check as an administrative end-of-project formality. It is the point at which the intervention's effects and learning are examined systematically so that the next decision is informed by more than the original plan or the preferences of the project team.
Study instruction:
- "For a hypothetical intervention facing a budget cut, identify what must be evaluated before stakeholders decide whether to continue."
Key point:
- A pause or decision point makes explicit evaluation and learning necessary.
- Resource constraints do not remove the need to understand consequences before deciding next steps.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
4. Q: How does the chapter define evaluation, and why is the definition not sufficient on its own for complex situations?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites the American Evaluation Association definition of evaluation as a systematic process for determining merit, worth, value or significance. This establishes that evaluation is concerned with judging the value of an intervention, not merely collecting performance data. However, the word systematic does not by itself specify whose values count, which boundaries are used, how competing purposes are handled, or how dynamic and non-linear effects are recognised. In complex situations, the evaluator must make these choices explicit and examine how different stakeholders experience the intervention. The chapter therefore argues for a systemic and developmental approach that can learn and adapt while assessing value.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the act of measuring an output from making a defensible judgment about an intervention's value."
Key point:
- Evaluation judges merit, worth, value or significance.
- The criteria, boundaries and perspectives used to make that judgment must also be examined.
Source links:
- American Evaluation Association: What Is Evaluation?
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
5. Q: Why does complexity make conventional evaluation models difficult to apply?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes situations marked by multiple causalities, non-linearity, dynamic change, plural perspectives and concerns about inequality, sustainability and environmental impact. A conventional model may assume that outcomes can be attributed to a limited set of inputs and that progress can be judged against stable targets. In a complex situation, effects may arise through interactions, emerge after a delay, or benefit one group while harming another. The context may also change while the evaluation is under way. A model that treats these conditions as noise can give an impoverished account of what happened. Systemic evaluation instead examines relationships, perspectives, boundaries and change, while remaining attentive to the values and interests embedded in the evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "Take one programme with changing conditions and identify three causal or contextual factors that a linear evaluation might miss."
Key point:
- Complexity involves interaction, non-linearity, changing context and plural values.
- Evaluation design must be appropriate to the reality being assessed.
Source links:
- American Evaluation Association: What Is Evaluation?
- De Souza (2022), A Critical Realist Approach to Systems Thinking in Evaluation
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
6. Q: What is meant by the chapter's criticism of an impoverished conversation between realities and evaluation models?
A: Show answer
The phrase, drawn from Reynolds and colleagues, describes a mismatch between the complexity of real situations and the simplified models used to evaluate them. Realities may be non-linear, contain attributes that cannot be precisely determined, and change as the context changes. If an evaluation model assumes stable variables, a single causal chain or fixed stakeholder interests, it cannot adequately represent those realities. The problem is not merely that the evaluator lacks enough data. The model may ask the wrong questions or exclude important relationships and values from the outset. A systemic approach tries to improve the conversation by adapting evaluation theory and methods to the situation rather than forcing the situation into a narrow template.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the assumptions of a fixed logic model with the features of a changing, multi-stakeholder situation."
Key point:
- A model can distort the situation when its assumptions do not fit the context.
- More data cannot fully compensate for an evaluation model with inappropriate boundaries or assumptions.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
- De Souza (2022), A Critical Realist Approach to Systems Thinking in Evaluation
7. Q: What is the single systems methodology approach to systemic evaluation?
A: Show answer
This approach applies a particular systems methodology to a defined evaluation purpose. The chapter traces its development through work bringing systems and evaluation practitioners together, especially the anthology Systems Concepts in Evaluation. Examples discussed include System Dynamics, cybernetic approaches, Soft Systems Methodology, Critical Systems Heuristics and complexity-based approaches. Later work also identifies the Viable System Model and Critical Systems Heuristics as useful sources of evaluation questions. A single methodology can provide a disciplined way to investigate a particular aspect of an intervention, such as organisational viability, stakeholder understanding or marginalised voices. Its contribution depends on the issue and context for which it is selected.
Study instruction:
- "Make a list of the systems methodologies named in the chapter and match each to a question it could help an evaluator ask."
Key point:
- A single systems methodology offers a focused evaluative lens and a related set of methods.
- Method selection should be justified by the evaluation question and context.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
8. Q: What value can individual systems methodologies add to evaluation practice?
A: Show answer
Individual methodologies can make evaluation more sensitive to aspects of a situation that conventional approaches may neglect. System Dynamics can help examine feedback and changing relationships among variables. Cybernetic thinking can draw attention to communication, regulation and control. Soft Systems Methodology can help explore different worldviews and develop mutual understanding. Critical Systems Heuristics can expose boundary judgments and ask whose interests are served or excluded. Complexity-based approaches can help evaluators consider emergence and adaptation. The chapter also discusses the use of the Viable System Model and CSH to generate useful evaluation questions. These are contributions rather than universal solutions: the evaluator must explain why a method fits the issue and what its use leaves outside the analysis.
Study instruction:
- "For a service redesign, choose two methodologies that would reveal different aspects of its effects and justify the choice."
Key point:
- A methodology contributes a structured way to see and investigate particular concerns.
- Its usefulness is contextual and should not be confused with universal coverage.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
- De Souza (2022), A Critical Realist Approach to Systems Thinking in Evaluation
9. Q: Why does the chapter argue that a single methodology is inadequate for evaluating a multimethodological CSP intervention?
A: Show answer
Every systems methodology embodies a partial worldview and directs attention toward some features of a situation more than others. If CSP deliberately combines methodologies and systemic perspectives to address general complexity, evaluating it through only one methodology risks judging the whole intervention through one partial lens. A method might show that efficiency improved while missing reduced adaptability, stakeholder conflict or harm to marginalised groups. The chapter invokes the idea that every perspective offers an adequate half-truth, but no one perspective should be mistaken for the whole truth. A more complete evaluation must draw on the range of systemic perspectives and use a multimethodological approach where the depth of inquiry requires it.
Study instruction:
- "Construct an example in which one evaluation lens reports success while another reveals a significant adverse effect."
Key point:
- A single lens can identify a real benefit while concealing other consequences.
- The evaluation of a multimethodological intervention should itself be multiperspectival and multimethodological.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
10. Q: What is the GEMS framework, and what does it illustrate about systemic evaluation?
A: Show answer
The chapter refers to the Gender equality, Environments and Marginalized voices framework developed for inclusive systemic evaluation in support of the Sustainable Development Agenda. It brings attention to dimensions that may be missed when evaluation focuses narrowly on aggregate performance or formal targets. The example illustrates how systems-informed evaluation can incorporate gender equality, environmental concerns and the experiences of marginalised groups. It is presented as an important contribution to evaluation practice, not as a complete substitute for every other systemic perspective or methodology. Its relevance to CSP is that evaluation must make visible whose experiences and interests are considered when judging the value of an intervention.
Study instruction:
- "Review a programme evaluation and identify how gender, environmental effects and marginalised voices are represented or omitted."
Key point:
- GEMS foregrounds gender equality, environments and marginalised voices.
- Inclusive evaluation asks whose outcomes and experiences are counted.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
11. Q: What is the systems concepts approach to evaluation?
A: Show answer
The systems concepts approach tries to make evaluation more systemic without requiring evaluators to learn and adopt a specific systems methodology. It identifies ideas thought to underpin many systems approaches and links them to existing evaluation methods. The chapter traces three concepts identified by Imam and colleagues: entangled systems, perspectives and boundaries. Williams later expresses them as understanding interrelationships, engaging with perspectives and reflecting on boundaries. Other writers add dynamics. The attraction is accessibility: evaluators can use these concepts as prompts for more thoughtful inquiry. The chapter's concern is that broad concepts may be too ambiguous to guide practice unless they are connected to the theories and methodologies from which their meanings arise.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a general concept such as boundaries could be used as an evaluation prompt and why further theoretical grounding may be needed."
Key point:
- The approach uses shared systems concepts as accessible prompts for evaluators.
- Accessibility is useful, but concepts need enough precision to guide actual inquiry.
Source links:
- Williams (2019), Systemic Evaluation Design: A Workbook
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
12. Q: Which systems concepts are emphasised in the systems concepts approach, and how are they expressed?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three foundational concepts: entangled systems, perspectives and boundaries. In Williams's later formulation, these become understanding interrelationships, engaging with perspectives and reflecting on boundaries. The Evaluation Topical Interest Group report also includes dynamics as a principle for effective use of systems thinking in evaluation, and Patton associates dynamics with the other concepts. Together, these ideas encourage evaluators to look beyond isolated activities, recognise that stakeholders see situations differently, examine what is included or excluded, and consider change over time. The chapter does not reject these prompts; it questions whether they can carry enough theoretical and methodological meaning when detached from the traditions that developed them.
Study instruction:
- "Apply interrelationships, perspectives, boundaries and dynamics to a single evaluation question and state what each adds."
Key point:
- The concepts encourage relational, plural, boundary-aware and dynamic inquiry.
- Their practical meaning depends on the systems tradition and the problem to which they are applied.
Source links:
- Williams (2019), Systemic Evaluation Design: A Workbook
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
13. Q: Why can the same systems concept mean different things in different systems traditions?
A: Show answer
Concepts are used within theories and practices that give them their meaning. The chapter illustrates this with interrelationships: a Viable System Model practitioner may focus on connections among vital subsystems and between an organisation and its environment, while a Soft Systems Methodology practitioner may focus on relationships among different worldviews. Boundaries also differ. A System Dynamics practitioner may define which variables belong in a model, whereas a Critical Systems Heuristics practitioner examines the factual and value judgments through which a boundary is drawn. The words may look shared, but the analytical commitments behind them are not identical. Evaluators should therefore ask which tradition informs a concept before treating it as a precise instruction.
Study instruction:
- "Take the terms interrelationship and boundary and write two different interpretations grounded in two methodologies named in the chapter."
Key point:
- Shared terminology does not guarantee shared theoretical meaning.
- Ask which systems tradition supplies the meaning before applying a concept.
Source links:
- Williams (2019), Systemic Evaluation Design: A Workbook
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
14. Q: What does the chapter's discussion of the linguistic turn imply for the use of systems concepts?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the linguistic turn to challenge the idea that a sign or concept has one fixed meaning independent of the language practices in which it is used. Drawing on Peirce and Wittgenstein, it argues that words function within broader systems of concepts and practices. The example of the word game shows that a familiar term can cover activities with no single defining feature shared by all of them. Applied to systems evaluation, this means that words such as perspective, boundary and relationship cannot safely be treated as self-explanatory. Their meaning must be interpreted in relation to the theory, methodology and practice in which they operate.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a word that seems familiar can still be analytically ambiguous in a professional framework."
Key point:
- Concepts acquire meaning through their wider conceptual and practical setting.
- Do not assume that familiar systems vocabulary has a single universal interpretation.
Source links:
- Williams (2019), Systemic Evaluation Design: A Workbook
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
15. Q: Why does Jackson describe detached systems concepts as potentially empty abstractions?
A: Show answer
A concept can become an empty abstraction when it is separated from the systemic perspective and methodological tradition that make it analytically useful. The chapter argues that simply telling evaluators to consider boundaries, perspectives or relationships may not challenge their existing assumptions. A practitioner may interpret each concept through a familiar mechanistic worldview and continue using a linear method. In that case, the language sounds systemic but does not lead to deeper engagement with complexity, pluralism or critical reflection. The concern is not that the concepts have no value; it is that their value is limited if evaluators are not shown how different traditions understand and use them.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether a proposed evaluation framework explains how its systems concepts change the evaluator's actual questions or methods."
Key point:
- Detached concepts may sound systemic without changing the evaluation practice.
- A useful concept must be connected to a clear theoretical and methodological application.
Source links:
- Williams (2019), Systemic Evaluation Design: A Workbook
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
16. Q: What is Walton and colleagues' criticism of the systems concepts approach?
A: Show answer
Walton and colleagues regard the systems concepts approach as a useful doorway into systems thinking but a weak long-term strategy if evaluators stop at broad prompts. Their review suggests that evaluators gain more value through deeper engagement with systems and complexity theories, methodologies and approaches. That engagement can help them assess the strengths and weaknesses of alternative methods, match methods to particular needs, refine them and develop methodological innovation. The chapter uses this argument to support the CST approach, which expects evaluators to engage more fully with the theories and methods associated with the systemic perspectives. The implication is that introductory concepts should lead to deeper practice rather than become a permanent substitute for it.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a short checklist of systems concepts with an evaluation design that explains its underlying theory and methods."
Key point:
- Concept prompts can open the door, but sustained capability requires deeper theoretical and methodological engagement.
- An evaluator should be able to explain why a method fits the issue and what assumptions it carries.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
- Williams (2019), Systemic Evaluation Design: A Workbook
17. Q: How does Critical Systems Thinking (CST) understand improvement and the purpose of evaluation?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects CST to Churchman and Ackoff's idea that improvement concerns progress by humankind toward an ideal. Evaluation should therefore examine whether an intervention has contributed to progress across the relevant aspects of that ideal. This is broader than asking whether a predetermined target was achieved. It requires attention to the values and purposes involved, including the interests of different stakeholders and the consequences of change. The ideal is not a single technical endpoint that can be fully specified in advance. It provides a direction for critical reflection and improvement. Check operationalises this commitment by evaluating the intervention through multiple systemic perspectives and considering what should happen next.
Study instruction:
- "Describe an organisational improvement goal as a direction for progress and identify whose values shape the judgment."
Key point:
- CST evaluates whether an intervention advances improvement toward an ideal.
- The ideal provides a critical direction, not a simplistic universal score.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
18. Q: How did the CST approach to systemic evaluation develop?
A: Show answer
The chapter traces an evolution from early attempts to relate evaluation approaches to different problem contexts toward a more explicitly complementary, multidimensional practice. Jackson and Medjedoub identified goal-based, organismic and multiple-actor approaches and related them to the System of Systems Methodologies. Gregory's work on voluntary service organisations developed a contingency approach and later examined four evaluation paradigms and their theoretical foundations. Further work highlighted the need to consider whether stakeholders were disadvantaged by an intervention. Gregory subsequently argued that different evaluation approaches should be used in parallel and connected through reflective conversation rather than theoretically integrated into one method. This development helped establish the foundation for CSP's developmental and multimethodological approach to evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "Create a short timeline from the early contingency approach to multidimensional evaluation and explain what changed."
Key point:
- CST evaluation evolved toward complementary use of distinct approaches.
- Plural methodologies can contribute together without being collapsed into a single theory.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
19. Q: What is multidimensional evaluation, and why does the chapter prefer it to theoretical integration?
A: Show answer
Multidimensional evaluation uses different methodologies in parallel and allows them to engage through reflective conversation. Gregory's argument, as presented in the chapter, is that the theoretical integration of distinct evaluation paradigms may be neither necessary nor desirable. Each paradigm has its own foundations and can make a distinctive contribution in a particular context. Using methodologies alongside one another allows evaluators to learn from their differences while avoiding the false impression that they have been merged into one coherent method. The result can be a more rounded evaluation, provided participants understand why each methodology is used and how its findings relate to the others.
Study instruction:
- "Design an evaluation using two complementary methods and specify how their findings would be discussed without pretending they are one method."
Key point:
- Multidimensional evaluation puts methodologies in parallel conversation.
- Complementarity preserves distinct theoretical contributions while supporting a fuller judgment.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
20. Q: What three activities make up the Check process?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three linked activities. First, evaluate the improvements achieved by examining the intervention through the systemic perspectives and, where needed, the methodologies associated with them. Second, reflect on the learning gained about the situation, CSP, the systemic perspectives and the individual systems approaches used. Third, discuss and agree on the next steps with decision-makers and stakeholders. The activities should not be treated as isolated boxes. Evaluation findings inform reflection, and both shape the next decision. A Check process that measures results but does not capture learning loses an important action-research benefit. A process that reflects but does not lead to a decision risks producing insight without a practical path forward.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a meeting agenda with one item for evaluation, one for learning and one for agreement on next steps."
Key point:
- Check combines evaluation, learning and forward decisions.
- The three activities form a connected decision process, not three unrelated reports.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
21. Q: Why should a CSP evaluation use all five systemic perspectives?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that each of the five perspectives reveals concerns that may be invisible from another perspective. An intervention can improve efficiency but weaken an organisation's ability to respond to its environment. A change may improve responsiveness yet create conflict if stakeholder purposes are ignored or implementation is forced through. A programme may raise social awareness but fail to meet essential service targets. A successful CSP intervention should therefore seek improvement across the mechanical, interrelationships, organismic, purposeful and societal/environmental perspectives. This does not mean every intervention will perform equally well on every criterion. It means evaluators should make the different consequences visible and discuss the trade-offs rather than allowing one perspective to dominate unnoticed.
Study instruction:
- "For a change initiative, write one evaluation question for each of the five perspectives before collecting outcome data."
Key point:
- Five perspectives provide a broader picture of improvement and unintended effects.
- A result that looks positive through one lens may look different through another.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
22. Q: What should evaluators examine through the mechanical systemic perspective?
A: Show answer
The mechanical perspective draws attention to efficiency and efficacy. In evaluation, it asks whether the intervention achieved its intended goals and whether resources and processes were used effectively to do so. The chapter recognises that this is a legitimate concern: an intervention should not ignore whether it delivers what it set out to achieve. The limitation arises when mechanical measures are treated as the whole evaluation. Targets can be met while interrelationships deteriorate, organisational viability weakens, stakeholder purposes are neglected or disadvantaged groups are harmed. Mechanical indicators should therefore be retained but interpreted alongside the other systemic perspectives.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two efficiency indicators and two efficacy indicators for a project, then identify what they would not reveal."
Key point:
- Mechanical evaluation tests goal achievement, efficiency and efficacy.
- Goal performance is necessary to examine but insufficient as a complete judgment of improvement.
Source links:
- Jackson (2023), Critical Systems Practice 4: Check
23. Q: How does the interrelationships perspective change the way intervention outcomes are evaluated?
A: Show answer
The interrelationships perspective asks how elements of the situation affect one another and whether an intervention has increased awareness of connections and unintended consequences. A change to one process can shift workloads, incentives, information flows or dependencies elsewhere. An evaluation that measures only the target activity may miss these effects. The chapter's examples show that a change intended to improve one feature of a system can create adverse consequences in another. Evaluators should therefore examine interactions among organisational units, stakeholders and the wider environment, including effects that were not anticipated when the intervention was designed.
Study instruction:
